# Daily Summary: 2025-04-20

### Subitizing-Inspired_Large_Language_Models_for_Floorplanning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12076v1)
- **Authors**: Shao-Chien Lu, Chen-Chen Yeh, Hui-Lin Cho, Yu-Cheng Lin, Rung-Bin Lin
- **Abstract**: We present a novel approach to solving the floorplanning problem by leveraging fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs). Inspired by subitizing--the human ability to instantly and accurately count small numbers of items at a glance--we hypothesize that LLMs can similarly address floorplanning challenges swiftly and accurately. We propose an efficient representation of the floorplanning problem and introduce a method for generating high-quality datasets tailored for model fine-tuning. We fine-tune LLMs on datasets with a specified number of modules to test whether LLMs can emulate the human ability to quickly count and arrange items. Our experimental results demonstrate that fine-tuned LLMs, particularly GPT4o-mini, achieve high success and optimal rates while attaining relatively low average dead space. These findings underscore the potential of LLMs as promising solutions for complex optimization tasks in VLSI design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to solving the floorplanning problem in VLSI design by leveraging fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs).  The core idea is inspired by the concept of "subitizing," the human ability to quickly recognize the number of items in a small set. The authors hypothesize that LLMs can similarly learn to quickly and accurately arrange modules in floorplanning.  The paper details a method for generating training datasets specifically for this purpose, involving recursive slicing and tree encoding. Experiments fine-tune several LLMs (including proprietary ones via the OpenAI API) on datasets of 16 and 24 modules and evaluates their performance based on success rate, optimal result rate, and dead space ratio. The results suggest that the fine-tuned LLMs, particularly GPT40-mini, can achieve high success and optimal rates with low average dead space, thus demonstrating the potential of LLMs for VLSI floorplanning. The paper acknowledges limitations and proposes future research directions including scaling to larger module counts, incorporating more comprehensive optimization objectives, and exploring other VLSI design challenges.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of fine-tuned LLMs to the floorplanning problem is relatively novel, although similar ideas are explored within other VLSI CAD applications. The inspiration from subitizing is a somewhat interesting conceptual hook. However, the technical approach itself is fairly straightforward, consisting of dataset generation through recursive slicing, standard LLM fine-tuning, and evaluation. The recursive slicing dataset generation, while practical, is not a fundamentally new contribution; similar techniques exist in related literature. The novelty lies primarily in the specific combination of LLMs with floorplanning and the particular dataset generation/encoding scheme chosen, but the core components have precedents.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates a proof-of-concept that LLMs can potentially be useful for tackling floorplanning, particularly for small to medium module counts. The achieved success and optimal rates for GPT40-mini are promising. However, the limitations significantly impact the potential significance. The approach is only evaluated on relatively small problems (16-24 modules), which are unlikely to represent realistic VLSI designs. It also neglects several critical constraints in floorplanning, such as wire length minimization, thermal management, and power consumption. The statement that the LLM output "should be integrated with a traditional floorplanner to provide guidance" reveals that the approach is *not* a complete replacement for existing floorplanning methods, further limiting its near-term impact. The limitation of not exploring costlier fine-tuning of larger models is also a weakness.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation of the approach.
    *   Detailed description of the dataset generation and fine-tuning process.
    *   Quantitative evaluation of multiple LLMs with relevant metrics.
    *   Identification of limitations and future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scale of the experiments (small module counts).
    *   Neglect of critical floorplanning constraints beyond dead space minimization.
    *   Reliance on relatively small/cost-efficient LLMs, preventing more rigorous investigation of model capacity.
    *   The LLM primarily acts as a pre-processor, suggesting rather incremental improvements.
    *  Lack of comparison against a comprehensive set of standard floorplanning algorithms.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could stimulate further research into using LLMs for VLSI CAD problems. Specifically, focusing LLMs to provide initial solutions or seed solutions to standard approaches could potentially yield results in more practical settings. However, the current study's lack of demonstrated benefit on practically sized problems and consideration of realistic constraints limits its immediate influence. The paper would benefit significantly from a comparison to existing floorplanning approaches, specifically the widely used Simulated Annealing.

*  **Overall Assessment:** This paper presents an interesting initial exploration of using LLMs for floorplanning but lacks the depth and comprehensiveness to demonstrate significant practical impact. While the idea has merit and the experiments provide some preliminary evidence, the limitations are substantial.

**Score: 5**

**Rigorous Rationale:**

I assign a score of 5 because the paper provides a novel but limited proof-of-concept. While it demonstrates that LLMs can be fine-tuned for the floorplanning problem and achieve some success on small datasets, the limited scope, lack of consideration for real-world constraints, and need for integration with a traditional floorplanner significantly reduce the novelty and significance of this contribution. It serves as a starting point, but significantly more research is needed to translate this into a practical and influential approach within the VLSI design field. The lack of comparison against other floorplanning methodologies further diminishes the paper's impact. A score of 5 represents an average contribution; the paper is competent but not transformative, and the impact is more of academic interest than practical application.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 5/10

### Selective Demonstration Retrieval for Improved Implicit Hate Speech Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12082v1)
- **Authors**: Yumin Kim, Hwanhee Lee
- **Abstract**: Hate speech detection is a crucial area of research in natural language processing, essential for ensuring online community safety. However, detecting implicit hate speech, where harmful intent is conveyed in subtle or indirect ways, remains a major challenge. Unlike explicit hate speech, implicit expressions often depend on context, cultural subtleties, and hidden biases, making them more challenging to identify consistently. Additionally, the interpretation of such speech is influenced by external knowledge and demographic biases, resulting in varied detection results across different language models. Furthermore, Large Language Models often show heightened sensitivity to toxic language and references to vulnerable groups, which can lead to misclassifications. This over-sensitivity results in false positives (incorrectly identifying harmless statements as hateful) and false negatives (failing to detect genuinely harmful content). Addressing these issues requires methods that not only improve detection precision but also reduce model biases and enhance robustness. To address these challenges, we propose a novel method, which utilizes in-context learning without requiring model fine-tuning. By adaptively retrieving demonstrations that focus on similar groups or those with the highest similarity scores, our approach enhances contextual comprehension. Experimental results show that our method outperforms current state-of-the-art techniques. Implementation details and code are available at TBD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of detecting implicit hate speech, which is more subtle and difficult to identify than explicit hate speech. The authors propose a novel method called Adaptive Retrieval-based In-context Learning (ARIIHA) that leverages in-context learning without requiring model fine-tuning. ARIIHA adaptively retrieves demonstrations based on two strategies: prioritizing demonstrations with similar target groups or selecting demonstrations with high similarity scores. The system dynamically determines the optimal retrieval strategy using thresholds derived from similarity scores and shortcut cues.  Experiments on the Implicit Hate Corpus (IHC) dataset demonstrate that ARIIHA outperforms existing state-of-the-art techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its adaptive retrieval mechanism for in-context learning in the context of implicit hate speech detection.  Previous work on in-context learning often relies on random sampling or simple similarity-based retrieval. ARIIHA's dynamic adaptation based on target groups and shortcut cues is a meaningful step forward.  The integration of BM25 retrieval with target group prioritization is also a potentially valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** Implicit hate speech detection is a crucial area because of its widespread and often insidious nature.  Methods that improve detection accuracy and reduce bias are of significant importance. The paper's claim of outperforming state-of-the-art techniques and mitigating over-sensitivity is a strong indication of its potential impact. The fact it does this *without* fine-tuning adds practical value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with detailed explanation of the ARIIHA framework.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on a standard dataset (IHC).
    *   Ablation studies to demonstrate the importance of each component of ARIIHA.
    *   Case studies to illustrate the system's successes and failures.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper relies on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, which while effective, might not be accessible to all researchers. Exploring other LLMs or open-source options would increase generalizability.
    *   The dynamic selection of retrieval strategy relies on thresholds. The process for determining these thresholds seems somewhat empirical (nested loop algorithm), and a more theoretically grounded approach could be beneficial.
    *   The paper mentions over-sensitivity as a key issue, but a deeper analysis of *why* ARIIHA reduces it compared to baseline methods would be valuable.  Is it because of better contextual understanding, reduced reliance on toxic keywords, or a combination of factors?
    *   The "shortcut cue" detection is based on 1-3 word phrases in quotes, which seems simplistic.  More sophisticated methods for identifying shortcut reasoning would strengthen the approach.
    *   The upper bound analysis is useful but leaves a non-negligible gap to be filled. This indicates there's room for further improvement and more refined methods.

*   **Impact:** The paper's approach has the potential to influence future research on in-context learning for hate speech detection and potentially other NLP tasks where nuanced contextual understanding is crucial. The idea of adaptively selecting demonstrations based on task-specific criteria could be generalized to other applications.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.  While the paper introduces a novel and potentially significant approach to implicit hate speech detection, there are areas for improvement. The reliance on specific LLM, the empirically driven threshold tuning, and the relatively simplistic shortcut cue detection slightly temper the overall impact. The paper demonstrably outperforms existing methods, but a more theoretically grounded analysis and exploration of alternative LLMs would significantly increase its value.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning-Based AI for Startup Evaluation (R.A.I.S.E.): A Memory-Augmented, Multi-Step Decision Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12090v1)
- **Authors**: Jack Preuveneers, Joseph Ternasky, Fuat Alican, Yigit Ihlamur
- **Abstract**: We present a novel framework that bridges the gap between the interpretability of decision trees and the advanced reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to predict startup success. Our approach leverages chain-of-thought prompting to generate detailed reasoning logs, which are subsequently distilled into structured, human-understandable logical rules. The pipeline integrates multiple enhancements - efficient data ingestion, a two-step refinement process, ensemble candidate sampling, simulated reinforcement learning scoring, and persistent memory - to ensure both stable decision-making and transparent output. Experimental evaluations on curated startup datasets demonstrate that our combined pipeline improves precision by 54% from 0.225 to 0.346 and accuracy by 50% from 0.46 to 0.70 compared to a standalone OpenAI o3 model. Notably, our model achieves over 2x the precision of a random classifier (16%). By combining state-of-the-art AI reasoning with explicit rule-based explanations, our method not only augments traditional decision-making processes but also facilitates expert intervention and continuous policy refinement. This work lays the foundation for the implementation of interpretable LLM-powered decision frameworks in high-stakes investment environments and other domains that require transparent and data-driven insights.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces R.A.I.S.E., a novel framework for evaluating startup success, leveraging large language models (LLMs) to combine reasoning capabilities with structured, rule-based decision-making. The framework uses chain-of-thought prompting to generate detailed reasoning logs, subsequently distilled into human-understandable logical rules. It incorporates various enhancements, including efficient data ingestion, two-step refinement, ensemble candidate sampling, reinforcement learning-based scoring, and persistent memory, to improve decision-making stability and transparency.  Experimental results on startup datasets demonstrate improved precision and accuracy compared to a standalone OpenAI model, highlighting the framework's potential for augmenting traditional investment processes and facilitating expert intervention.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The combination of LLM-based reasoning with structured rule extraction is a key novel aspect.  While prior work has explored LLMs for startup evaluation (Founder-GPT, GPTREE), and enhancing LLM transparency (chain-of-thought, ensembling), R.A.I.S.E. integrates these techniques in a specific pipeline optimized for explainability and expert oversight. The use of reinforcement learning and persistent memory is a valuable innovation that further improves stability and interpretability. However, these techniques are not wholly new, and other fields have explored similar mechanisms.
* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the "black box" problem of LLMs in high-stakes decision-making environments like venture capital. The framework's explainability and editability empower stakeholders to understand, override, and refine the underlying policies, potentially leading to greater trust and more effective investment strategies. The ability to trace decisions back to explicit rules is valuable for auditing and compliance purposes. However, the experimental results, while positive, are based on a limited dataset and demonstrate only incremental improvements with each added component.
* **Strengths:**
    *   Clear framework design and modular architecture.
    *   Integration of state-of-the-art LLM techniques for reasoning and explanation.
    *   Emphasis on human interpretability and expert intervention.
    *   Experimental validation demonstrating improved performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Relatively small dataset size used for evaluation (limiting generalizability).
    *   Incremental improvements with each component, suggesting diminishing returns and raising questions about complexity vs. benefit.
    *   Lack of comparison with other state-of-the-art models for startup success prediction (e.g., gradient boosting or random forests trained on a combination of structured and unstructured data), making it difficult to fully assess its competitive advantage.
    *   Limited discussion on the challenges of extracting robust and reliable rules from LLM outputs, particularly in the presence of noise and inconsistencies.
    *   Reliance on OpenAI's models raises concerns about API costs and potential limitations in scalability. The dependence on proprietary models may not align with the goal of creating a widely accessible, interpretable solution.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more transparent and accountable AI systems for decision-making in various domains. It could inspire further research on combining LLMs with structured knowledge representations and incorporating expert feedback into the AI pipeline.
*   **Score Justification:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by proposing a novel framework for explainable AI in startup evaluation. The integration of different LLM techniques and the emphasis on human interpretability are commendable. However, the limited experimental validation and lack of comparison with existing models limit its overall impact. A few other works have similarly touched this area, making this work one of the many building onto existing works. Therefore, the novelty, while present, is not significantly groundbreaking.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Gauging Overprecision in LLMs: An Empirical Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12098v1)
- **Authors**: Adil Bahaj, Hamed Rahimi, Mohamed Chetouani, Mounir Ghogho
- **Abstract**: Recently, overconfidence in large language models (LLMs) has garnered considerable attention due to its fundamental importance in quantifying the trustworthiness of LLM generation. However, existing approaches prompt the \textit{black box LLMs} to produce their confidence (\textit{verbalized confidence}), which can be subject to many biases and hallucinations. Inspired by a different aspect of overconfidence in cognitive science called \textit{overprecision}, we designed a framework for its study in black box LLMs. This framework contains three main phases: 1) generation, 2) refinement and 3) evaluation. In the generation phase we prompt the LLM to generate answers to numerical questions in the form of intervals with a certain level of confidence. This confidence level is imposed in the prompt and not required for the LLM to generate as in previous approaches. We use various prompting techniques and use the same prompt multiple times to gauge the effects of randomness in the generation process. In the refinement phase, answers from the previous phase are refined to generate better answers. The LLM answers are evaluated and studied in the evaluation phase to understand its internal workings. This study allowed us to gain various insights into LLM overprecision: 1) LLMs are highly uncalibrated for numerical tasks 2) {\color{blue}there is no correlation between the length of the interval and the imposed confidence level, which can be symptomatic of a a) lack of understanding of the concept of confidence or b) inability to adjust self-confidence by following instructions}, {\color{blue}3)} LLM numerical precision differs depending on the task, scale of answer and prompting technique {\color{blue}4) Refinement of answers doesn't improve precision in most cases}. We believe this study offers new perspectives on LLM overconfidence and serves as a strong baseline for overprecision in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an empirical study on gauging overprecision in Large Language Models (LLMs). It introduces a novel framework to assess overprecision by prompting LLMs to generate numerical intervals at specified confidence levels, refining these answers using aggregation and self-refinement techniques, and then evaluating performance using cognitive science-inspired metrics (hit rate and correlation between interval length and confidence).  The core idea is to examine if LLMs can accurately generate intervals reflecting their internal uncertainty about numerical answers. The study reveals that LLMs are poorly calibrated for numerical tasks, lack a clear understanding of confidence, and exhibit variable precision depending on the task and answer scale. Furthermore, refinement strategies, particularly self-refinement, often fail to improve performance and can even degrade it.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its adoption of *overprecision* from cognitive science as a lens to analyze LLM overconfidence. Existing works primarily focus on *overestimation* or direct confidence elicitation, which are known to be susceptible to biases and hallucinations. The approach of *imposing* a confidence level and then evaluating if the generated interval aligns with it is innovative.  The framework itself, with its distinct generation, refinement, and evaluation phases tailored for numerical answers, is a valuable contribution.  The detailed exploration of aggregation methods for numerical intervals and contrasting those results with traditional self-probing techniques is also relatively new.

**Significance:**  The findings have important implications for understanding the trustworthiness of LLMs, particularly in scenarios requiring numerical reasoning and estimations of uncertainty. The observation that refinement strategies can sometimes backfire is significant and warrants further investigation. Highlighting the dissociation between stated confidence and actual precision challenges the common practice of relying on LLM confidence scores without careful evaluation. This has practical relevance for applications like financial analysis or medical diagnostics, where miscalibrated uncertainty estimates can lead to critical errors. The paper establishes a strong baseline and sets the stage for future research to explore the underlying causes of overprecision and develop more effective mitigation strategies. The paper's methodological contributions (dataset creation and specific cognitive-inspired metrics) are also valuable for future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The experimental framework is well-designed and systematically evaluates different prompting strategies, refinement techniques, and confidence levels.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the research questions, methodology, results, and implications.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The study considers a variety of datasets, models, and evaluation metrics, providing a comprehensive picture of overprecision in LLMs.
*   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** Grounding the work in cognitive science concepts provides a strong theoretical basis for the research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Selection:**  The study mainly focuses on GPT-3.5-turbo and GPT-4-0-mini. While these are popular models, expanding the evaluation to include other LLM architectures would enhance the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Dataset Bias:** While the datasets were filtered for numerical answers, inherent biases in the original datasets might still influence the results.  A more detailed analysis of potential dataset biases would be beneficial. The study acknowledges limitations in the datasets, which is positive.
*   **Hinting Bias**: As described in Section B, the hinting may cause the LLM to produce "artificially" improved answers. A more "distant" hinting approach would provide a more rigorous assessment of LLM's overprecision.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Shifting the focus of overconfidence research in LLMs from overestimation to overprecision.
*   Providing a robust framework for evaluating uncertainty in numerical reasoning tasks.
*   Motivating the development of more calibrated LLMs for critical applications.
*   Encouraging further research on the underlying causes of overprecision.

The paper, by demonstrating that current techniques are insufficient for building robust confidence estimates, calls for better methods in the field and therefore has high potential for driving future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed study on an important aspect of LLM reliability. The adoption of overprecision from cognitive science is a significant strength. While the study has some limitations, its contributions to understanding and measuring LLM overconfidence are substantial. The paper's findings are likely to stimulate further research and inform the development of more trustworthy AI systems, particularly in numerical contexts. The points raised in the "Weaknesses" section prevented a score higher than 7.5, as addressing them could have resulted in an even stronger and more impactful contribution. The hinting also potentially reduced the overall value of the paper. A future study without this potentially biasing method could be valuable.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generalized Visual Relation Detection with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12100v1)
- **Authors**: Kaifeng Gao, Siqi Chen, Hanwang Zhang, Jun Xiao, Yueting Zhuang, Qianru Sun
- **Abstract**: Visual relation detection (VRD) aims to identify relationships (or interactions) between object pairs in an image. Although recent VRD models have achieved impressive performance, they are all restricted to pre-defined relation categories, while failing to consider the semantic ambiguity characteristic of visual relations. Unlike objects, the appearance of visual relations is always subtle and can be described by multiple predicate words from different perspectives, e.g., ``ride'' can be depicted as ``race'' and ``sit on'', from the sports and spatial position views, respectively. To this end, we propose to model visual relations as continuous embeddings, and design diffusion models to achieve generalized VRD in a conditional generative manner, termed Diff-VRD. We model the diffusion process in a latent space and generate all possible relations in the image as an embedding sequence. During the generation, the visual and text embeddings of subject-object pairs serve as conditional signals and are injected via cross-attention. After the generation, we design a subsequent matching stage to assign the relation words to subject-object pairs by considering their semantic similarities. Benefiting from the diffusion-based generative process, our Diff-VRD is able to generate visual relations beyond the pre-defined category labels of datasets. To properly evaluate this generalized VRD task, we introduce two evaluation metrics, i.e., text-to-image retrieval and SPICE PR Curve inspired by image captioning. Extensive experiments in both human-object interaction (HOI) detection and scene graph generation (SGG) benchmarks attest to the superiority and effectiveness of Diff-VRD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diff-VRD, a novel approach for visual relation detection (VRD) using diffusion models.  Instead of traditional discriminative classification, Diff-VRD models visual relations as continuous embeddings and utilizes a diffusion model to generate all possible relations conditioned on subject-object pair features.  It addresses the semantic ambiguity inherent in visual relations by modeling the diffusion process in a latent space. A matching stage assigns relation words based on semantic similarity.  To evaluate the generalized VRD task (beyond predefined datasets), the authors introduce new metrics: text-to-image retrieval and a SPICE-inspired precision-recall curve.  Experiments on HOI detection and SGG benchmarks demonstrate the superiority of Diff-VRD, especially in generating diverse and reasonable relations not present in standard datasets.  The method can also be used as a post-processing step to improve other VRD models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its application of diffusion models to the VRD task. This is a significant departure from the typical discriminative classification approaches used in the field.  Modeling relations as continuous embeddings is also innovative in this context. The introduction of proxy tasks/metrics tailored to evaluate the quality of *generalized* VRD outputs is a valuable contribution. The authors make a compelling case that traditional VRD evaluation metrics don't properly capture the essence of more open-ended relation prediction. However, diffusion models are increasingly common in various generative tasks, and while the specific application to VRD is new, the fundamental building blocks are relatively standard.

*   **Significance:**  VRD is a crucial component of scene understanding. By addressing the semantic ambiguity of relations and allowing for the discovery of novel interactions, Diff-VRD has the potential to advance AI agents' ability to comprehend complex scenes.  The new evaluation metrics offer a way forward for assessing models that go beyond fixed relation vocabularies. The ability to enhance existing VRD models via post-processing offers immediate practical utility.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing Semantic Ambiguity:** Diff-VRD directly tackles a significant limitation of existing VRD models.
    *   **Generative Approach:** Using a diffusion model provides flexibility and the potential to discover novel relations.
    *   **New Evaluation Metrics:** Text-to-image retrieval and the SPICE PR curve provide a more comprehensive evaluation than traditional metrics.
    *   **Performance:** Experimental results show improved performance in terms of discovering relations beyond the fixed dataset categories, and a T2I is improved with synonym expansion.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Diffusion Model Complexity:** Diffusion models are computationally expensive, and the paper doesn't fully address the computational burden associated with Diff-VRD.  The paper provides the number of training epochs but fails to compare the latency of inference as an enhancement with competing methods, as well as whether it is real-time performant.
    *   **Dependence on Object Detection:** The approach still relies on an external object detector, inheriting any limitations or biases present in that detector.  However, this is standard within the current research paradigm in VRD, and the authors state it explicitly.
    *   **Qualitative Examples are Limited:**  The paper would benefit from more qualitative examples, particularly of "novel" relations that the model discovers and how these contribute to overall scene understanding. It would also be beneficial to include more cases of the model failing when using text-to-image retrieval, or other failure cases.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work could encourage further exploration of generative models for VRD and a shift away from purely discriminative approaches.  The proposed evaluation metrics might become standard for assessing generalized VRD models. It offers a promising path towards more flexible and comprehensive scene understanding systems.

**Justification:**

The paper offers a solid contribution by using diffusion models for VRD. However, its novelty is incremental since diffusion models themselves are well-established. Moreover, the paper could further strengthen the contributions of the diffusion model. The introduction of text-to-image retrieval and SPICE PR curve has a positive impact, and the performance demonstrates benefits.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Entropy-Guided Watermarking for LLMs: A Test-Time Framework for Robust and Traceable Text Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12108v1)
- **Authors**: Shizhan Cai, Liang Ding, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has intensified concerns about content traceability and potential misuse. Existing watermarking schemes for sampled text often face trade-offs between maintaining text quality and ensuring robust detection against various attacks. To address these issues, we propose a novel watermarking scheme that improves both detectability and text quality by introducing a cumulative watermark entropy threshold. Our approach is compatible with and generalizes existing sampling functions, enhancing adaptability. Experimental results across multiple LLMs show that our scheme significantly outperforms existing methods, achieving over 80\% improvements on widely-used datasets, e.g., MATH and GSM8K, while maintaining high detection accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel watermarking scheme for Large Language Models (LLMs) that aims to improve both detectability and text quality compared to existing methods.  The key idea is to introduce a cumulative watermark entropy threshold.  Text is only watermarked once a certain level of "entropy" (uncertainty or randomness) is reached in the generated output. When the threshold is crossed, preceding tokens act as a seed to generate a key, which is then used to watermark the text. This adaptively controls the watermarking process, leading to improved text quality in cases where deterministic outputs or few-shot templates would otherwise be negatively impacted. The method is compatible with, and extends, existing sampling functions.  Experiments demonstrate significant improvements in performance on long answer QA datasets, such as MATH and GSM8K, while maintaining high detection accuracy, even under paraphrase attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using an entropy threshold to control the watermarking process is the key novel contribution.  This provides a mechanism for adaptive watermarking that addresses a key weakness of previous methods: their inflexibility in situations where high-quality text requires more deterministic output, such as few-shot examples. Also, the theoretical validation of indistinguishability further strengthens the novelty and rigor of the proposed approach.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses an important problem in LLM research: balancing the need for robust watermarking with the preservation of text quality. The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements on challenging datasets, indicating the practical significance of the proposed scheme. The fact that it generalizes to different sampling strategies and LLMs adds to its impact. Watermarking for LLMs is a field of increasing importance due to concerns about misuse and copyright, so this contribution is timely and relevant.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Adaptive Watermarking:** The entropy threshold is a clever mechanism that improves the trade-off between detectability and text quality.
    * **Robustness:** The method demonstrates robustness to paraphrase attacks, a key weakness of some existing techniques.
    * **Generalizability:** It works across different LLMs and sampling methods, showcasing its adaptability.
    * **Theoretical Grounding:** Includes a theoretical argument for indistinguishability.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** Significant performance improvements on challenging QA datasets are compelling.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** The paper is technically dense, which may limit its accessibility.  Simplifying the explanations and providing more intuitive illustrations could improve readability.
    * **Parameter Tuning:** The paper does not fully explore the sensitivity of the method to the choice of the entropy threshold parameter (λ). How to choose optimal λ based on task or LLM characteristics could be discussed.
    * **Scalability for incredibly long sequences:** Although the experiments indicate a successful approach, the paper would benefit from addressing potential scalability issues with very long sequences and the computational cost of entropy estimation and key generation in such scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM watermarking. The entropy-guided approach is a novel and effective way to improve both detectability and text quality. The theoretical analysis and thorough experimental results support the claims. While the technical complexity and limited exploration of parameter tuning are minor drawbacks, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The achieved improvements on key datasets highlight the practical benefits of the proposed method and its potential to address the pressing challenges of content traceability in the era of large language models.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Diffusion-Based Framework for Terrain-Aware Remote Sensing Image Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12112v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Yu, Mohd Yamani Inda Idris, Pei Wang
- **Abstract**: Remote sensing imagery is essential for environmental monitoring, agricultural management, and disaster response. However, data loss due to cloud cover, sensor failures, or incomplete acquisition-especially in high-resolution and high-frequency tasks-severely limits satellite imagery's effectiveness. Traditional interpolation methods struggle with large missing areas and complex structures. Remote sensing imagery consists of multiple bands, each with distinct meanings, and ensuring consistency across bands is critical to avoid anomalies in the combined images. This paper proposes SatelliteMaker, a diffusion-based method that reconstructs missing data across varying levels of data loss while maintaining spatial, spectral, and temporal consistency. We also propose Digital Elevation Model (DEM) as a conditioning input and use tailored prompts to generate realistic images, making diffusion models applicable to quantitative remote sensing tasks. Additionally, we propose a VGG-Adapter module based on Distribution Loss, which reduces distribution discrepancy and ensures style consistency. Extensive experiments show that SatelliteMaker achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SatelliteMaker, a diffusion-based framework designed to reconstruct missing data in remote sensing images, addressing challenges such as cloud occlusion, sensor failures, and data acquisition issues. The method tackles both spatial gaps within a single image and temporal gaps across time series data. SatelliteMaker leverages Digital Elevation Models (DEMs) as conditioning input, enabling controlled content generation through tailored prompts for quantitative remote sensing applications. A VGG-Adapter module, based on distribution loss, reduces distribution discrepancy and ensures style consistency in reconstructed images. The authors conduct experiments across multiple tasks and datasets, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance in maintaining spatial, spectral, and temporal consistency. The paper proposes a dataset and promises to release the code upon acceptance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a critical problem:** Remote sensing image reconstruction is a crucial task with significant applications in environmental monitoring, agriculture, and disaster response. The paper tackles a real-world problem.
    *   **Novel approach:** Leveraging diffusion models for remote sensing image reconstruction is a relatively underexplored area. Integrating DEM data as conditional inputs and using tailored prompts represents a step towards applying diffusion models to more quantitative tasks.
    *   **Demonstrated Performance:** The experiments appear to show that SatelliteMaker outperforms existing methods in terms of PSNR, RMSE, SSIM, and LPIPS. The ablation study further validates the contribution of the VGG-Adapter module.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper undertakes comprehensive experiments across different datasets (Landsat-8, EarthNet2021) and evaluation metrics. The consideration of both spatial and temporal data completion is a positive aspect.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Core Diffusion Architecture:** While the application to remote sensing is interesting, the paper might not introduce fundamentally novel contributions to the core diffusion model architecture itself. The novelty primarily lies in the application and adaptations for remote sensing data. The adaptations made to the base model appear to be somewhat standard.
    *   **Dependency on DEM data:** The reliance on DEM data can be a limiting factor in some applications where such data isn't readily available or is of poor quality. The paper could discuss the limitations of relying on DEM data and explore alternative conditioning methods.
    *   **Limited Description of VGG-Adapter:** The paper describes the VGG-Adapter module, but lacks in-depth details about its architecture and implementation. More clarification on the specific layers used, the training process, and the rationale behind the architecture is needed.
    *   **Dataset Issues:** The filtering of the Landsat-8 data seems too strict (cloud cover < 1%). This severely limits the size of the dataset and may not represent real-world conditions where satellite images are often affected by significant cloud cover. Also, both tasks involve dataset cleaning by removing cloudy data, implying the model can't fully handle cases when cloud cover is *not* removed.
    *   **Unconvincing Visualizations:** A few of the visualizations in figures 4 and 5 don't look particularly convincing. Also, the "Ground Truth" in many visual examples exhibits noticeable contrast enhancements. In figure 5, the colors in various examples of "interpolate" appear to be off compared with the "ground truth."
    *   **High computational cost:** It should be explicitly stated whether the method is extremely computationally intensive, due to the usage of diffusion models.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper has the potential to advance the field of remote sensing image reconstruction by offering a more effective solution for handling missing data, especially in challenging scenarios.
    *   The integration of DEM data opens possibilities for more accurate and context-aware reconstruction, aligning generated content with physical ground truth.
    *   The VGG-Adapter contributes to better color consistency and distribution alignment, addressing a common issue in image inpainting tasks.
    *   The approach could have broad implications for improving the usability of satellite imagery in various applications.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, the paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of remote sensing. The core idea of using a diffusion model and conditioning it with DEM data is sound. The results presented show that this architecture performs well, but they aren't overwhelmingly better. The model depends too much on pre-cleaned data. Also, the novelty of the work is incremental rather than groundbreaking.

- **Score**: 6/10

### Clarifying Ambiguities: on the Role of Ambiguity Types in Prompting Methods for Clarification Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12113v1)
- **Authors**: Anfu Tang, Laure Soulier, Vincent Guigue
- **Abstract**: In information retrieval (IR), providing appropriate clarifications to better understand users' information needs is crucial for building a proactive search-oriented dialogue system. Due to the strong in-context learning ability of large language models (LLMs), recent studies investigate prompting methods to generate clarifications using few-shot or Chain of Thought (CoT) prompts. However, vanilla CoT prompting does not distinguish the characteristics of different information needs, making it difficult to understand how LLMs resolve ambiguities in user queries. In this work, we focus on the concept of ambiguity for clarification, seeking to model and integrate ambiguities in the clarification process. To this end, we comprehensively study the impact of prompting schemes based on reasoning and ambiguity for clarification. The idea is to enhance the reasoning abilities of LLMs by limiting CoT to predict first ambiguity types that can be interpreted as instructions to clarify, then correspondingly generate clarifications. We name this new prompting scheme Ambiguity Type-Chain of Thought (AT-CoT). Experiments are conducted on various datasets containing human-annotated clarifying questions to compare AT-CoT with multiple baselines. We also perform user simulations to implicitly measure the quality of generated clarifications under various IR scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of ambiguity in user queries within information retrieval (IR) and proposes a new prompting scheme called AMBIGUITY TYPE-CHAIN OF THOUGHT (AT-CoT) for generating clarifying questions (CQs).  The key idea is to explicitly model and integrate ambiguity types (ATs) into the reasoning process of Large Language Models (LLMs) when generating clarifications. The AT taxonomy is action-based, consisting of "Semantic," "Generalize," and "Specify." AT-CoT prompts LLMs to first predict the relevant ATs for a given query, and then generate CQs based on those ATs. The method is evaluated on clarification generation datasets (Qulac, ClariQ, RaoCQ) and IR datasets (TREC Web track, TREC DL Hard) using both intrinsic (clarification generation) and extrinsic (information retrieval via user simulation) tasks. Results show that AT-CoT consistently outperforms standard prompting, CoT prompting, and a baseline that only provides AT definitions, demonstrating the benefits of ambiguity-oriented reasoning for clarification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**
    * **Strength:** The primary novelty lies in the explicit modeling and integration of ambiguity types into the reasoning process of LLMs for clarification generation. While CoT prompting has been explored previously, this paper provides a structured way of constraining CoT's reasoning to specific ambiguity types before generating clarifying questions.  The development of an actionable AT taxonomy is also a valuable contribution, as existing taxonomies are primarily analytical.
    * **Weakness:** The action-based AT taxonomy, while useful, is relatively simple (Semantic, Generalize, Specify).  The connection to previous work on ambiguity types is somewhat limited, as the paper focuses on a very high-level categorization.  The impact is somewhat limited by the simplicity of the taxonomy.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** The improvement over vanilla CoT, while consistent, isn't groundbreaking. The reported gains are statistically significant but not dramatically large in magnitude.

* **Significance:**
    * **Strength:** The paper addresses an important problem in IR: how to effectively handle ambiguous user queries and engage users in a conversational search process.  The use of LLMs for clarification generation is a relevant and active research area.  The study contributes to the growing body of work on proactive dialogue systems and provides empirical evidence for the benefits of ambiguity-aware clarification. The user simulation experiments offer a practical way of evaluating the downstream impact of better clarification generation.
    * **Weakness:** The impact is limited by the relatively simple user simulation.  Assuming cooperative users without intent shifts is a significant simplification.  The reliance on facet-specific document relevance judgements for TREC datasets simplifies user intent modeling, which might not translate well to more realistic search scenarios.
    * **Reproducibility:** The paper gives reasonable details about the implementation and experimental setup which could enable reproducing the primary outcomes of the work.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed study with a clear research question and tangible results.  However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, building on existing CoT prompting methods.  The significance is also moderate, as the user simulation, while useful, relies on simplifying assumptions. The contributions have value for researchers working on conversational IR and LLM prompting, but don't necessarily represent a paradigm shift. A higher score would have required a more substantial novelty, a deeper exploration of ambiguity types, and more realistic user simulations.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Anti-Aesthetics: Protecting Facial Privacy against Customized Text-to-Image Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12129v1)
- **Authors**: Songping Wang, Yueming Lyu, Shiqi Liu, Ning Li, Tong Tong, Hao Sun, Caifeng Shan
- **Abstract**: The rise of customized diffusion models has spurred a boom in personalized visual content creation, but also poses risks of malicious misuse, severely threatening personal privacy and copyright protection. Some studies show that the aesthetic properties of images are highly positively correlated with human perception of image quality. Inspired by this, we approach the problem from a novel and intriguing aesthetic perspective to degrade the generation quality of maliciously customized models, thereby achieving better protection of facial identity. Specifically, we propose a Hierarchical Anti-Aesthetic (HAA) framework to fully explore aesthetic cues, which consists of two key branches: 1) Global Anti-Aesthetics: By establishing a global anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and a global anti-aesthetic loss, it can degrade the overall aesthetics of the generated content; 2) Local Anti-Aesthetics: A local anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and a local anti-aesthetic loss are designed to guide adversarial perturbations to disrupt local facial identity. By seamlessly integrating both branches, our HAA effectively achieves the goal of anti-aesthetics from a global to a local level during customized generation. Extensive experiments show that HAA outperforms existing SOTA methods largely in identity removal, providing a powerful tool for protecting facial privacy and copyright.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called Hierarchical Anti-Aesthetic (HAA) to protect facial privacy against customized text-to-image synthesis. Recognizing that existing methods overlook the crucial role of aesthetics in human perception of image quality, HAA aims to degrade the generation quality of maliciously customized models from an aesthetic perspective. The framework comprises two key branches: Global Anti-Aesthetics (GAA), which degrades the overall aesthetics of generated content, and Local Anti-Aesthetics (LAA), which disrupts local facial identity. By seamlessly integrating both branches, HAA aims to achieve anti-aesthetics from a global to local level. The authors demonstrate that HAA outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in identity removal, effectively safeguarding facial privacy and copyright.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in its aesthetic-driven approach to facial privacy protection within the context of customized text-to-image synthesis. While adversarial attacks are common for anti-customization, utilizing aesthetic qualities is a fresh angle. The hierarchical decomposition (global and local aesthetics) provides a structured way to exploit perceptual cues. The idea of creating *anti*-aesthetic images, guiding adversarial noise to reduce rather than improve quality is conceptually sound and represents a notable departure from traditional adversarial methods that are designed for robustness against adversarial attacks, not to enhance their power.

**Significance:** The proliferation of customized diffusion models poses a serious threat to privacy. If the proposed method can effectively disrupt malicious use, it's a valuable contribution. The empirical results presented show promising performance compared to SOTA methods. Successfully eliminating facial identity is crucial for preventing misuse. The potential to protect both personal privacy and copyright further broadens the significance. If the approach extends beyond simple face swapping to preventing more sophisticated misuse cases it would be highly significant.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach of using anti-aesthetic principles is a unique and intriguing perspective in privacy protection.
*   **Structured Framework:** The hierarchical design (global and local aesthetics) is well-organized and allows for targeted disruption of image generation.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments demonstrate that HAA outperforms existing SOTA methods in identity removal.
*   **Potential Impact:** The work has the potential to be a valuable tool for protecting facial privacy and copyright in the era of customized diffusion models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Datasets:** The method relies on aesthetic preference datasets (VisionRewardDB-Image) and pre-trained models (BLIP, face detection model). The performance is directly related to the biases and limitations present in these components. There is limited discussion on bias inherent in image aesthetics and whether anti-aesthetics might unfairly impact particular demographic groups, or create biases during data collection for training datasets.
*   **Limited generalizability:** The paper tests HAA with three prompts but how well it functions when a person is situated in a more complex scene is not clear. Also, there is no mention of how the prompts were selected and there could be further room to explore robustness with a more diverse set of prompts.
*   **Potential for Evasion:** It's unclear how resistant HAA is to more sophisticated attacks that might specifically target its anti-aesthetic design (e.g., adversarial training). Future work is warranted to explore evasion techniques.
*   **Stealth Implications:** While the paper asserts that the adversarial noise is subtle, there needs to be a better discussion about the trade-off between privacy protection and image usability. If HAA significantly degrades the perceptual quality of the original image, it becomes less useful in practice.

**Justification for the Score:**

Despite the weaknesses, the paper has several strengths that indicate a novel and significant contribution. The aesthetic-driven approach is innovative, and the hierarchical framework provides a structured and effective methodology. The empirical results, showing substantial improvements over existing methods, add confidence to its practical significance. However, the reliance on pre-existing data, and the lack of discussion on biases, as well as the potential for evasion, and a need for deeper investigation into the usability/privacy trade-offs limits the overall impact. The paper is not revolutionary but provides a novel and practical advancement to privacy protection. It represents a clear, worthwhile, novel step forward.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Multilingual Contextualization of Large Language Models for Document-Level Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12140v1)
- **Authors**: Miguel Moura Ramos, Patrick Fernandes, Sweta Agrawal, André F. T. Martins
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance in sentence-level machine translation, but scaling to document-level translation remains challenging, particularly in modeling long-range dependencies and discourse phenomena across sentences and paragraphs. In this work, we propose a method to improve LLM-based long-document translation through targeted fine-tuning on high-quality document-level data, which we curate and introduce as DocBlocks. Our approach supports multiple translation paradigms, including direct document-to-document and chunk-level translation, by integrating instructions both with and without surrounding context. This enables models to better capture cross-sentence dependencies while maintaining strong sentence-level translation performance. Experimental results show that incorporating multiple translation paradigms improves document-level translation quality and inference speed compared to prompting and agent-based methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a method to improve document-level machine translation using large language models (LLMs). The core idea is to fine-tune LLMs on a newly curated, high-quality document-level dataset called DOCBLOCKS. This dataset contains full documents and contextual segments from various domains. The authors propose a fine-tuning approach that supports multiple translation paradigms, including direct document-to-document translation and chunk-level translation, using instructions with and without surrounding context. The paper demonstrates that their fine-tuning method, combined with prompt-based context modeling, improves document-level translation quality and inference speed compared to prompting and agent-based methods.  They experiment with several LLMs and benchmark against state-of-the-art systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of the DOCBLOCKS dataset and the specific fine-tuning methodology tailored for document-level MT. While fine-tuning LLMs for MT is not new, the focus on high-quality document-level data and the multi-paradigm fine-tuning approach offers a valuable contribution. The incorporation of context-aware prompt tuning (CAPT) and multi-resolutional document-to-document training (MRD2D) also contribute to the methodology. The comprehensive comparison against prompting and agent-based methods is valuable.

*   **Significance:** Document-level MT remains a challenging problem, and the paper addresses a critical need by providing a dataset and fine-tuning approach specifically designed for this task. The results demonstrate tangible improvements in document-level translation quality, making the work relevant to the MT community. The finding that fine-tuning can outperform prompting and agent-based methods for document-level MT is significant, as it offers a more efficient and potentially scalable solution. The analysis of different chunking strategies and their impact on translation quality also provides insights into how to optimize document-level translation pipelines. The ablation studies provide additional value by highlighting the importance of filtering DOCBLOCKS and using CAPT/MRD2D.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   High-quality dataset: DOCBLOCKS addresses a data scarcity issue in document-level MT.
    *   Comprehensive experimentation: The authors experiment with several LLMs, datasets, and baselines, providing a robust evaluation of their approach.
    *   Clear methodology: The fine-tuning approach and training strategies are well-defined.
    *   Ablation studies: Ablation results demonstrate the importance of various components.
    *   Competitive performance: The results show substantial improvements over sentence-level models and good performance compared to other document-level methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Dataset size constraints: The paper acknowledges that the dataset curation process resulted in limiting the size of the datasets used.
    *   Metric Limitations: It explicitly mentions the limitations of evaluation metrics, which affects the overall assessment of document-level quality improvements.
    *   Limited novelty in fine-tuning techniques: Fine-tuning LLMs is a well-established technique, although its application to this specific problem and in this way has merit.

*   **Potential Impact:** The DOCBLOCKS dataset and fine-tuning approach have the potential to become a valuable resource for the MT community, enabling further research and development in document-level MT. The findings could influence the design of future MT systems, favoring fine-tuning over more complex prompting or agent-based strategies.

**Justification:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to the field of document-level MT, primarily driven by the dataset. While the individual components (fine-tuning, prompt tuning) are not ground-breaking, the specific combination and application to the document-level MT problem, along with the curated dataset, provides significant value.  The paper's rigor and comprehensive evaluation strengthen its conclusions. However, limitations with evaluation metrics and the fact that it is building on top of other research areas prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution.

### Mapping Controversies Using Artificial Intelligence: An Analysis of the Hamas-Israel Conflict on YouTube
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12177v1)
- **Authors**: Victor Manuel Hernandez Lopez, Jaime E. Cuellar
- **Abstract**: This article analyzes the Hamas-Israel controversy through 253,925 Spanish-language YouTube comments posted between October 2023 and January 2024, following the October 7 attack that escalated the conflict. Adopting an interdisciplinary approach, the study combines the analysis of controversies from Science and Technology Studies (STS) with advanced computational methodologies, specifically Natural Language Processing (NLP) using the BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) model. Using this approach, the comments were automatically classified into seven categories, reflecting pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, anti- Palestinian, anti-Israeli positions, among others. The results show a predominance of pro- Palestinian comments, although pro-Israeli and anti-Palestinian comments received more "likes." This study also applies the agenda-setting theory to demonstrate how media coverage significantly influences public perception, observing a notable shift in public opinion, transitioning from a pro- Palestinian stance to a more critical position towards Israel. This work highlights the importance of combining social science perspectives with technological tools in the analysis of controversies, presenting a methodological innovation by integrating computational analysis with critical social theories to address complex public opinion phenomena and media narratives.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Mapping Controversies Using Artificial Intelligence: An Analysis of the Hamas-Israel Conflict on YouTube" investigates the Hamas-Israel conflict using natural language processing (NLP) on Spanish-language YouTube comments.  It classifies over 250,000 comments posted between October 2023 and January 2024 into seven categories reflecting stances (pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, etc.). The study combines Science and Technology Studies (STS) methodologies with computational techniques (BERT model) to analyze the controversy.  The results reveal a predominance of pro-Palestinian comments, although pro-Israeli comments received more "likes". The paper also applies agenda-setting theory to examine how media coverage influences public opinion, noting a shift from pro-Palestinian sentiments to a more critical view of Israel over time.  The authors argue for combining social science perspectives with technological tools for controversy analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Interdisciplinary Approach:** The paper successfully integrates STS and computational methods, offering a more holistic approach than solely relying on one discipline.
    *   **Timeliness and Relevance:** The study addresses a highly relevant and ongoing global conflict, analyzing public discourse in a timely manner.
    *   **Large Dataset:** The use of a large dataset (250,000+ comments) lends statistical power to the findings.
    *   **Contextualization:** The authors effectively contextualize the computational analysis with a historical overview of the conflict and an examination of media coverage, strengthening the interpretation of results.
    *   **Novel Combination of Theories:** Combining controversy mapping methodologies from STS with agenda-setting theory provides a framework for understanding the dynamic interaction between social media discourse, media narratives, and public opinion.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Language Limitation:** Focusing solely on Spanish-language comments limits the generalizability of the findings to other linguistic and cultural contexts.
    *   **Potential Biases:** While the paper acknowledges the potential for biases in BERT and mentions "cleaning" the data, a more detailed discussion of how biases were mitigated and their potential impact on the results is needed.
    *   **Categorization Challenges:**  The study's inability to accurately classify "Anti-Hamas" comments is a significant weakness. The overlap with other categories implies that some data may be incorrectly classified, thereby skewing the representation of the different viewpoints. A more robust category selection process is needed.
    *   **Oversimplification:**  While the study aims to mediate between STS and machine learning, it tends to oversimplify the social, historical, and political context. For example, its claim that media coverage is the primary factor behind a shift in public opinion ignores other potential factors, such as grassroots movements, political campaigns, and international relations.
    *   **Limited Discussion of "Likes":** While it notes that pro-Israeli comments receive more "likes" than pro-Palestinian comments, this warrants further exploration. Why do pro-Israeli comments garner more "likes," and what does this reveal about the biases within YouTube's recommendation algorithms?

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper demonstrates a functional application of BERT for classifying opinions surrounding the conflict on YouTube. However, classifying sentiments and opinions on social media is a relatively established area of research.
    *   The real value comes from its combination of NLP with STS framework for better interpretability. This may influence further researches on the dynamics of online sentiments.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The study could encourage further research on the use of computational methods in conjunction with social science theories for analyzing online controversies.
    *   It provides insights into the dynamics of online discourse surrounding the Hamas-Israel conflict.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable, timely analysis of a complex global issue, using a combination of computational methods and social science theories. However, its limitations in language scope, potential biases, and categorization challenges, and the oversimplification of media influence somewhat temper its impact. Additionally, while the methodologies are not entirely new, their specific application and interpretability are commendable.
