### Multimodal LLM Augmented Reasoning for Interpretable Visual Perception Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12511v1)
- **Authors**: Shravan Chaudhari, Trilokya Akula, Yoon Kim, Tom Blake
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we advance the study of AI-augmented reasoning in the context of Human-Computer Interaction (HCI), psychology and cognitive science, focusing on the critical task of visual perception. Specifically, we investigate the applicability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in this domain. To this end, we leverage established principles and explanations from psychology and cognitive science related to complexity in human visual perception. We use them as guiding principles for the MLLMs to compare and interprete visual content. Our study aims to benchmark MLLMs across various explainability principles relevant to visual perception. Unlike recent approaches that primarily employ advanced deep learning models to predict complexity metrics from visual content, our work does not seek to develop a mere new predictive model. Instead, we propose a novel annotation-free analytical framework to assess utility of MLLMs as cognitive assistants for HCI tasks, using visual perception as a case study. The primary goal is to pave the way for principled study in quantifying and evaluating the interpretability of MLLMs for applications in improving human reasoning capability and uncovering biases in existing perception datasets annotated by humans.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to analyze and interpret visual perception, focusing on explainability and human-computer interaction.  Instead of building a new predictive model for visual complexity, it uses MLLMs as cognitive assistants, leveraging principles from psychology and cognitive science (Gestalt principles, visual clutter, symmetry) to benchmark MLLMs' ability to assess visual content. The authors use a novel annotation-free framework with carefully designed prompts to guide the MLLMs. They demonstrate a correlation between MLLM-assessed explainable attributes and human-annotated complexity ratings using datasets like SAVIOAS and IC9600, highlighting potential biases in human perception.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using MLLMs to *explain* visual complexity based on established psychological principles, rather than just *predicting* it with deep learning, represents a degree of novelty. The annotation-free framework is a strength as it sidesteps dataset bias inherent in methods trained with labeled data. However, the novelty is somewhat limited.  Applying LLMs/MLLMs to reasoning and explainability is a growing trend; thus, the specific application of using them to dissect visual perception, while interesting, is not entirely groundbreaking. There isn't a strong technical innovation, as the method primarily relies on prompt engineering.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to provide a more transparent and accountable approach to AI-driven visual analysis. By quantifying the alignment of MLLM reasoning with human cognitive principles, the work could pave the way for more trustworthy AI systems in HCI. The identified biases in human-annotated datasets are valuable insights for researchers working with these datasets. The work provides a framework that researchers can build upon to explore how the human-AI loop improves human reasoning and visual analysis. However, the study's impact is constrained by its reliance on a single MLLM (Claude Sonnet 3.0) for its primary experiments. The generalizability of the findings to other MLLMs remains an open question.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Annotation-free framework:** Avoids dataset biases that plague supervised learning approaches.
    *   **Emphasis on explainability:** Focuses on *why* an image is perceived as complex, not just *how* complex it is.
    *   **Connection to established theory:**  Grounds the work in psychological and cognitive science principles.
    *   **Identification of dataset biases:** Reveals potential limitations in existing human-annotated visual complexity datasets.
    *   Prompt design is clear and well-justified.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited MLLM diversity:** Primarily relies on Claude Sonnet 3.0; generalizability not fully addressed.
    *   **Lack of a strong technical contribution:** Primarily prompt engineering. More sophisticated prompting techniques could be explored, for instance using chain-of-thought reasoning.
    *   **Correlation vs. Causation:**  The paper demonstrates correlations but doesn't delve into the causal relationships between the explainable attributes and human complexity perception, which would strengthen the analysis.
    *   While the framework aims to benchmark MLLMs' ability to reason, the reliance on human ratings for validation makes the framework susceptible to similar biases that the framework aims to mitigate.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper could influence how visual complexity and similar perceptual tasks are approached in HCI research. It advocates for a move beyond simple prediction towards more interpretable and cognitively grounded AI systems.  It offers a methodology that can be extended to other perceptual tasks and types of AI systems.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to evaluating MLLMs for visual perception analysis by using a reasoning-based, annotation-free framework grounded in established psychological principles.  It provides useful insights into the biases present in existing datasets. However, the lack of diverse MLLM experimentation, limited technical innovation (primarily prompt engineering), and lack of causal analysis weakens the paper's overall contribution. While the paper is a valuable step in a promising direction, its significance and potential impact are not exceptionally high to warrant a higher rating. The paper is a solid contribution but could benefit from further technical development and more extensive validation across a range of MLLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evaluating the Diversity and Quality of LLM Generated Content
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12522v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Shypula, Shuo Li, Botong Zhang, Vishakh Padmakumar, Kayo Yin, Osbert Bastani
- **Abstract**: Recent work suggests that preference-tuning techniques--including Reinforcement Learning from Human Preferences (RLHF) methods like PPO and GRPO, as well as alternatives like DPO--reduce diversity, creating a dilemma given that such models are widely deployed in applications requiring diverse outputs. To address this, we introduce a framework for measuring effective semantic diversity--diversity among outputs that meet quality thresholds--which better reflects the practical utility of large language models (LLMs). Using open-ended tasks that require no human intervention, we find counterintuitive results: although preference-tuned models--especially those trained via RL--exhibit reduced lexical and syntactic diversity, they produce greater effective semantic diversity than SFT or base models, not from increasing diversity among high-quality outputs, but from generating more high-quality outputs overall. We discover that preference tuning reduces syntactic diversity while preserving semantic diversity--revealing a distinction between diversity in form and diversity in content that traditional metrics often overlook. Our analysis further shows that smaller models are consistently more parameter-efficient at generating unique content within a fixed sampling budget, offering insights into the relationship between model scaling and diversity. These findings have important implications for applications that require diverse yet high-quality outputs, from creative assistance to synthetic data generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the tension between diversity and quality in LLM-generated content, particularly after preference tuning (e.g., PPO, DPO).  It introduces a framework for measuring "effective semantic diversity" – diversity among outputs meeting a quality threshold. The authors find that while preference tuning reduces lexical and syntactic diversity, it can *increase* effective semantic diversity by producing more high-quality outputs overall, even if those high-quality outputs are more semantically similar to each other.  They also observe that smaller models can be more parameter-efficient in generating unique content and that preference tuning impacts syntactic diversity differently than lexical diversity.  The paper uses code generation as its testing ground, leveraging program execution to assess semantic equivalence and validity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a valuable refinement to how diversity is measured in LLM outputs.  The focus on *effective semantic diversity* is a significant improvement over simplistic lexical or neural metrics. The counterintuitive findings regarding preference tuning and its impact on diversity beyond surface-level differences are novel. The examination of model size and parameter efficiency in generating unique outputs is another interesting contribution. However, the use of code generation, while rigorous, limits the direct applicability and generalizability of the findings to other domains like creative writing or story generation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for applications requiring diverse, high-quality content, like synthetic data generation or creative assistance. The insight that preference tuning, often associated with reduced diversity, can actually increase effective semantic diversity by improving overall quality challenges conventional wisdom. Identifying the different effects of preference tuning and model scaling on diversity of form (syntax, lexical) vs. diversity of content (semantics) is impactful. This knowledge can inform better strategies for training LLMs tailored to specific application needs. The framework proposed can be used as a benchmark for comparing the diversity of content generated by different models in a variety of open-ended tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The "effective semantic diversity" metric is a strong conceptual contribution.
    *   The use of program execution as a proxy for semantic equivalence and validity is a rigorous approach.
    *   The counterintuitive findings are well-supported by empirical evidence.
    *   The paper tackles a practically relevant problem (generating diverse, high-quality content).
    *   The identification of distinct effects of different training techniques and model sizes on diversity.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The focus on code generation limits the generalizability of the findings. While the paper makes a compelling argument for code's suitability for analysis, it's unclear how these results translate directly to other tasks.
    *   The definition of "semantic equivalence" through program execution, while rigorous, may be too strict or narrow in some contexts. Subtleties in semantic meaning that don't affect program output are missed.
    *   The dataset size (108 prompts) is reasonable, but a larger and more varied set of prompts would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   The paper assumes (and implements) that test cases are sufficient to find edge-case differences, but the degree to which this assumption affects diversity is untested.
    *   The reliance on code generation might narrow the readership of the paper to researchers with backgrounds in programming languages or code generation.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research on diversity measurement in LLMs, especially those focused on code generation or applications where objective quality metrics exist. The framework presented could become a standard way to measure and compare LLM content diversity for specific tasks. The insights about preference tuning and model scaling should inform training strategies and model selection in practical applications.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by refining the notion of "diversity" for LLMs and providing empirical evidence to support its framework. The findings are practically relevant and challenge existing assumptions. However, the limitation to code generation, the strict definition of semantic equivalence, and dataset size somewhat limit the broader impact and generalizability of the research. The research provides novel insights and a useful methodology, moving beyond traditional surface-level analyses of diversity. Given the described strengths and weaknesses, a moderate score is justified.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Memorization vs. Reasoning: Updating LLMs with New Knowledge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12523v1)
- **Authors**: Aochong Oliver Li, Tanya Goyal
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) encode vast amounts of pre-trained knowledge in their parameters, but updating them as real-world information evolves remains a challenge. Existing methodologies and benchmarks primarily target entity substitutions, failing to capture the full breadth of complex real-world dynamics. In this paper, we introduce Knowledge Update Playground (KUP), an automatic pipeline for simulating realistic knowledge updates reflected in an evidence corpora. KUP's evaluation framework includes direct and indirect probes to both test memorization of updated facts and reasoning over them, for any update learning methods. Next, we present a lightweight method called memory conditioned training (MCT), which conditions tokens in the update corpus on self-generated "memory" tokens during training. Our strategy encourages LLMs to surface and reason over newly memorized knowledge at inference. Our results on two strong LLMs show that (1) KUP benchmark is highly challenging, with the best CPT models achieving $<2\%$ in indirect probing setting (reasoning) and (2) MCT training significantly outperforms prior continued pre-training (CPT) baselines, improving direct probing (memorization) results by up to $25.4\%$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of updating Large Language Models (LLMs) with new knowledge, focusing on how to improve their ability to memorize and reason about evolving real-world information.  The authors introduce the Knowledge Update Playground (KUP), an automatic pipeline for creating realistic but fictitious knowledge updates for LLM training. KUP includes an evaluation framework with direct and indirect probes to test both memorization and reasoning. They also propose a lightweight training method called memory conditioned training (MCT) where "memory" tokens are generated and prepended to training data, encouraging LLMs to leverage their existing knowledge when learning updates. Experiments on open-source LLMs demonstrate that KUP is challenging for existing methods and MCT outperforms continued pre-training baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes several contributions, but its overall novelty is limited. While the KUP framework and MCT training method address a crucial problem, they don't represent a radical departure from existing techniques. The core of the idea builds on the existing work of continued pre-training and attempts to improve it with knowledge conditioning. The use of fictitious data is a reasonable choice in building a repeatable testbed, yet also a limitation.

Here's a breakdown of the strengths and weaknesses:

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a practically relevant problem:** Keeping LLMs up-to-date is a significant challenge in a constantly changing world, and this paper directly targets this issue.
    *   **KUP framework:** The automatic generation of knowledge updates and associated test sets is a valuable resource. This simplifies the process of evaluating update methods, and generating training data. The inclusion of *indirect probing* to measure reasoning is important.
    *   **MCT method:** The memory conditioned training approach is sensible and demonstrates empirical gains over CPT, showing some promise for improving knowledge integration.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimentation with two strong open-source LLMs, and releases code and data, which makes results more reliable and actionable for the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty:** The method is incrementally novel, building on the well-established continued pre-training approach. While the "memory token" conditioning aspect has some appeal, it's not a breakthrough innovation.
    *   **Artificial Data:** While fictitious data enables consistent evaluation, it can not fully encapsulate the complexities of real-world knowledge updates, making it more difficult to generalize results. It would be important to compare performance on real, evolving data.
    *   **Indirect Probing Performance:**  The indirect probing task is difficult.  Achieving such low results (< 2%) highlight the challenge but also suggests limited success with reasoning overall.
    *   **Explanation for CoT Improvements:** While MCT better leverages CoT, the exact mechanism behind this improvement is not clearly explained.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**
    * The paper highlights the limitations of current CPT methods.
    * The KUP framework provides a good start for benchmarking update methods, and could inspire future research on this topic.
    * The MCT method, while not a groundbreaking advance, presents a simple way to improve CPT, and its effectiveness makes it a useful baseline for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above, the paper represents a solid contribution to the field, but it does not significantly alter the trajectory of research or introduce revolutionary concepts. The practicality of the approach and the release of the KUP framework are valuable assets, but the limitations relating to the artificial data and the incremental innovation need to be taken into account.

### Generalization through variance: how noise shapes inductive biases in diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12532v1)
- **Authors**: John J. Vastola
- **Abstract**: How diffusion models generalize beyond their training set is not known, and is somewhat mysterious given two facts: the optimum of the denoising score matching (DSM) objective usually used to train diffusion models is the score function of the training distribution; and the networks usually used to learn the score function are expressive enough to learn this score to high accuracy. We claim that a certain feature of the DSM objective -- the fact that its target is not the training distribution's score, but a noisy quantity only equal to it in expectation -- strongly impacts whether and to what extent diffusion models generalize. In this paper, we develop a mathematical theory that partly explains this 'generalization through variance' phenomenon. Our theoretical analysis exploits a physics-inspired path integral approach to compute the distributions typically learned by a few paradigmatic under- and overparameterized diffusion models. We find that the distributions diffusion models effectively learn to sample from resemble their training distributions, but with 'gaps' filled in, and that this inductive bias is due to the covariance structure of the noisy target used during training. We also characterize how this inductive bias interacts with feature-related inductive biases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the generalization capabilities of diffusion models, focusing on how these models can generate samples beyond their training data, despite the common understanding that the standard denoising score matching (DSM) objective optimizes for the training data's score function, and the models used have sufficient capacity to memorize the training data.  The authors propose the key insight that the noise inherent in the DSM objective – specifically the difference between the "proxy score" (the target during training) and the true score – plays a critical role in promoting generalization. They develop a mathematical theory, using a physics-inspired path integral approach (Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism), to characterize the distributions learned by under- and overparameterized diffusion models.  The theory suggests that diffusion models sample from distributions resembling the training data, but with "gaps" filled in, and this is attributed to the covariance structure of the noisy training target. The paper analyzes how this 'generalization through variance' interacts with other inductive biases, like model features and architecture.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its articulation of "generalization through variance" as a central mechanism for diffusion model behavior. While the effect of noise in training has been acknowledged in prior work (e.g., the observation by Karras et al., 2022 about loss weighting, and Xu et al., 2023 on the need for variance reduction), the authors offer a deeper theoretical framework for understanding how this noise shapes the learned distribution and promotes generalization. The use of the MSR path integral to analyze this is a noteworthy contribution.

*   **Significance:** If the theoretical framework holds, the paper has significant implications for understanding and controlling the inductive biases of diffusion models. It provides a lens through which to analyze how training procedures, model architectures, and even the training data itself impact generalization capabilities. This could lead to better designs for diffusion models that are more robust, controllable, and capable of genuine out-of-distribution generation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper offers a mathematically rigorous treatment of a challenging problem. The use of path integrals provides a sophisticated tool for analyzing the learned distributions.
    *   **Clear Explanation of Key Concepts:** The paper clearly articulates the concept of the "proxy score" and its covariance structure, and how this relates to the learned distributions.
    *   **Connection to Empirical Observations:** The paper attempts to connect its theoretical findings to existing empirical observations in the literature.
    *   **Discussion of Limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their work, such as the simplification of the training dynamics and the focus on unconditional models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Tractability vs. Realism:** To achieve analytical tractability, the paper makes several simplifying assumptions (e.g., about model architectures and training dynamics). The applicability of the results to more complex, realistic diffusion models is not entirely clear.
    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The theoretical framework is well-developed, but the paper lacks extensive empirical validation. While there are references to existing empirical findings, directly testing the theoretical predictions through targeted experiments would strengthen the claims. The figures are interesting, but are based on rather simple examples. A more systematic comparison between predictions and observations would significantly improve the paper.
    *   **Complexity:** The mathematical formalism may be difficult for a broader audience to grasp. While rigor is valuable, greater effort towards making the key ideas accessible would enhance the paper's impact.
    *   **Lack of Codebase:** The manuscript mentions code that reproduces the figures. Making the codebase available would be extremely helpful for the community to validate the results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on diffusion models by providing a new theoretical perspective on generalization. It could inspire new training techniques, architecture designs, and methods for analyzing and controlling the behavior of diffusion models. However, this hinges on the ability of others to build upon and extend the proposed framework, and verify the results through experiments.

*   **Justification of Score:**  The paper presents a novel theoretical contribution to the understanding of generalization in diffusion models. While the simplifications made for analytical tractability and the limited empirical validation slightly diminish its immediate impact, the rigor and insights offered warrant a higher score. The physics-inspired approach using path integrals is a sophisticated tool and offers a new perspective on the problem. However, greater efforts toward accessibility and making the theory more directly testable would substantially enhance the paper's influence.

### Knowledge Acquisition on Mass-shooting Events via LLMs for AI-Driven Justice
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12545v1)
- **Authors**: Benign John Ihugba, Afsana Nasrin, Ling Wu, Lin Li, Lijun Qian, Xishuang Dong
- **Abstract**: Mass-shooting events pose a significant challenge to public safety, generating large volumes of unstructured textual data that hinder effective investigations and the formulation of public policy. Despite the urgency, few prior studies have effectively automated the extraction of key information from these events to support legal and investigative efforts. This paper presented the first dataset designed for knowledge acquisition on mass-shooting events through the application of named entity recognition (NER) techniques. It focuses on identifying key entities such as offenders, victims, locations, and criminal instruments, that are vital for legal and investigative purposes. The NER process is powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) using few-shot prompting, facilitating the efficient extraction and organization of critical information from diverse sources, including news articles, police reports, and social media. Experimental results on real-world mass-shooting corpora demonstrate that GPT-4o is the most effective model for mass-shooting NER, achieving the highest Micro Precision, Micro Recall, and Micro F1-scores. Meanwhile, o1-mini delivers competitive performance, making it a resource-efficient alternative for less complex NER tasks. It is also observed that increasing the shot count enhances the performance of all models, but the gains are more substantial for GPT-4o and o1-mini, highlighting their superior adaptability to few-shot learning scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of extracting key information from unstructured text related to mass-shooting events to support AI-driven justice. It introduces a new dataset designed for knowledge acquisition on these events, focusing on named entity recognition (NER) to identify entities like offenders, victims, locations, and criminal instruments.  The NER process leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) such as GPT-3.5, GPT-4o, and 01-mini using a few-shot prompting approach. The experimental results, conducted on a real-world mass-shooting corpus, suggest that GPT-4o is the most effective model for this task, while 01-mini provides a resource-efficient alternative for less complex NER. The study demonstrates that increasing the number of shots (few-shot examples) improves performance, particularly for GPT-4o and 01-mini. The paper claims to be the first dataset specifically designed for knowledge acquisition on mass-shooting events using NER.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's claim of introducing the first dataset specifically for knowledge acquisition on mass-shooting events via NER is a significant contribution.  While NER itself is not a novel technique, its application to this specific domain and the creation of a specialized dataset enhance its potential impact.
*   **Significance:** The application of AI to improve justice-related processes, especially in the context of mass shootings, has considerable societal importance. Automating the extraction of relevant information from unstructured data can significantly aid investigations, policy-making, and predictive analyses.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly defines the problem and provides a compelling motivation for the research.
    *   The dataset creation is a valuable contribution, as it provides a resource for future research in this area.
    *   The use of few-shot learning with LLMs is a practical approach, given the limited availability of labeled data in this domain.
    *   The comparative evaluation of different LLMs provides valuable insights into their suitability for this task.
    *   The analysis of the impact of the number of shots on performance offers practical guidance for optimizing the NER pipeline.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies heavily on OpenAI's GPT models, which are proprietary and may not be accessible to all researchers.  This limits the reproducibility and generalizability of the findings.
    *   While the paper presents performance metrics, it lacks a deeper qualitative analysis of the errors made by the LLMs. Understanding the types of errors and their potential impact on the downstream applications would enhance the paper's value.
    *   The experiments could benefit from a comparison to existing NER methods (other than LLMs) to further demonstrate the benefits of the proposed approach.
    *   There is minimal discussion on the potential ethical considerations of using AI for justice-related purposes, particularly regarding bias in the data and algorithms.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in AI-driven justice, particularly in the area of mass-shooting investigations. The dataset and the insights into LLM performance can serve as a starting point for future studies.
*   **Missing Ethical Considerations:** The study should acknowledge the potential for bias in the data used to train the LLMs. Mass shooting data might reflect existing societal biases, leading to unfair or discriminatory outcomes if the AI system is used for predictive policing or suspect profiling.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by creating a specialized dataset and evaluating LLMs for NER on mass-shooting events. The research is well-motivated and has the potential to impact AI-driven justice. However, the reliance on proprietary models, lack of in-depth error analysis, absence of comparative performance analysis, and minimal discussion of ethical concerns limit its overall impact. The paper introduces relevant considerations in AI-driven justice, however more research is needed in regards to ethical frameworks and implications of AI tools in the legal process.

### ELAB: Extensive LLM Alignment Benchmark in Persian Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12553v1)
- **Authors**: Zahra Pourbahman, Fatemeh Rajabi, Mohammadhossein Sadeghi, Omid Ghahroodi, Somaye Bakhshaei, Arash Amini, Reza Kazemi, Mahdieh Soleymani Baghshah
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation framework for aligning Persian Large Language Models (LLMs) with critical ethical dimensions, including safety, fairness, and social norms. It addresses the gaps in existing LLM evaluation frameworks by adapting them to Persian linguistic and cultural contexts. This benchmark creates three types of Persian-language benchmarks: (i) translated data, (ii) new data generated synthetically, and (iii) new naturally collected data. We translate Anthropic Red Teaming data, AdvBench, HarmBench, and DecodingTrust into Persian. Furthermore, we create ProhibiBench-fa, SafeBench-fa, FairBench-fa, and SocialBench-fa as new datasets to address harmful and prohibited content in indigenous culture. Moreover, we collect extensive dataset as GuardBench-fa to consider Persian cultural norms. By combining these datasets, our work establishes a unified framework for evaluating Persian LLMs, offering a new approach to culturally grounded alignment evaluation. A systematic evaluation of Persian LLMs is performed across the three alignment aspects: safety (avoiding harmful content), fairness (mitigating biases), and social norms (adhering to culturally accepted behaviors). We present a publicly available leaderboard that benchmarks Persian LLMs with respect to safety, fairness, and social norms at: https://huggingface.co/spaces/MCILAB/LLM_Alignment_Evaluation.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces ELAB, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating the alignment of Persian Large Language Models (LLMs) with ethical dimensions like safety, fairness, and social norms. The authors address a critical gap in existing LLM evaluation frameworks, which are often tailored to English and fail to capture the nuances of Persian language, culture, and ethical considerations. ELAB comprises three types of Persian-language benchmarks: translated data (Anthropic Red Teaming, AdvBench, HarmBench, DecodingTrust), synthetically generated data (ProhibiBench-fa, SafeBench-fa, FairBench-fa, SocialBench-fa), and naturally collected data (GuardBench-fa). The authors evaluate Persian LLMs across safety, fairness, and social norms, and provide a public leaderboard.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper addresses a significant and timely problem: the lack of culturally-sensitive alignment benchmarks for LLMs in languages other than English. The core novelty lies in the creation of a comprehensive Persian-specific benchmark that considers the unique linguistic structures, cultural norms (like "taarof" and "aberoo"), and ethical considerations relevant to Persian-speaking contexts.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** ELAB encompasses a diverse range of datasets, including translated, synthetically generated, and naturally collected data, providing a holistic evaluation framework.
*   **Culturally Grounded:** The newly created datasets address harmful and prohibited content specific to indigenous culture, a crucial aspect often overlooked in English-centric benchmarks. The integration of Persian cultural norms, such as deference to authority and social dignity, is a significant strength.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the methodology for dataset creation, including translation, adaptation, and generation processes.
*   **Practical Implementation:** The creation of a unified framework and leaderboard facilitates transparent comparison between Persian LLMs.
*   **Focus on Interdependencies:** The work attempts to address interdependencies between safety, fairness, and norms, moving beyond treating them as isolated aspects.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on GPT-4o:** The synthetic data generation heavily relies on Command-R Plus and GPT-4o for classification. While these models are powerful, the benchmark's validity is inherently tied to the biases and limitations of these generative models. A more diverse approach to synthetic data generation and verification would strengthen the results.
*   **Limited Details on Data Collection:** The description of the GuardBench-fa data collection process is somewhat vague. More details on the specific sources and filtering criteria would improve the reproducibility and reliability of this dataset.
*   **Lack of In-depth Analysis:** While the paper presents performance comparisons, the analysis could delve deeper into the specific types of errors and biases exhibited by different models. Understanding *why* certain models perform better or worse in certain areas would be more valuable than simply presenting scores.
*   **Subjectivity in Social Norms:** Defining and evaluating social norms is inherently subjective. The paper could benefit from a more rigorous discussion of the potential biases and limitations in defining and assessing these norms.
* **Model Choices and Evaluation Metric:** The chosen model pool (Ministral, Qwen, Gemma and related Instruct and chat models) for evaluation is rather limited in scope. More models would have provided a better and more reliable overview.

**Significance and Potential Impact:**

ELAB represents a significant step towards responsible development of LLMs in non-English languages. By providing a culturally sensitive benchmark, the paper enables researchers and developers to better understand and mitigate potential biases and harms in Persian LLMs. The publicly available dataset and leaderboard can foster further research and development in this area. The methodology used in creating this benchmark can be adapted to other low-resource languages.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a critical gap in LLM alignment for a specific language. The comprehensive benchmark and culturally grounded datasets are significant strengths. However, the reliance on particular generative models, limited data collection details, lack of in-depth error analysis, and the inherent subjectivity in social norm evaluation slightly detract from the overall impact. The potential to adapt the methodology to other languages and its contribution to responsible AI development justify a score of 7, indicating a noteworthy contribution with room for further improvement. More extensive testing with more and recent models would have raised the score by at least 1.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmarking LLM-based Relevance Judgment Methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12558v1)
- **Authors**: Negar Arabzadeh, Charles L. A. Clarke
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in both academic and industry settings to automate the evaluation of information seeking systems, particularly by generating graded relevance judgments. Previous work on LLM-based relevance assessment has primarily focused on replicating graded human relevance judgments through various prompting strategies. However, there has been limited exploration of alternative assessment methods or comprehensive comparative studies. In this paper, we systematically compare multiple LLM-based relevance assessment methods, including binary relevance judgments, graded relevance assessments, pairwise preference-based methods, and two nugget-based evaluation methods~--~document-agnostic and document-dependent. In addition to a traditional comparison based on system rankings using Kendall correlations, we also examine how well LLM judgments align with human preferences, as inferred from relevance grades. We conduct extensive experiments on datasets from three TREC Deep Learning tracks 2019, 2020 and 2021 as well as the ANTIQUE dataset, which focuses on non-factoid open-domain question answering. As part of our data release, we include relevance judgments generated by both an open-source (Llama3.2b) and a commercial (gpt-4o) model. Our goal is to \textit{reproduce} various LLM-based relevance judgment methods to provide a comprehensive comparison. All code, data, and resources are publicly available in our GitHub Repository at https://github.com/Narabzad/llm-relevance-judgement-comparison.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive benchmark and comparison of various Large Language Model (LLM)-based relevance judgment methods for information retrieval (IR) evaluation. It systematically compares binary relevance, graded relevance, pairwise preference, and two nugget-based approaches (document-agnostic and document-dependent) using both a commercial LLM (GPT-4o) and an open-source LLM (Llama3.2b) on datasets from TREC Deep Learning tracks and the ANTIQUE dataset. The comparison focuses on two key factors: alignment with human labels and agreement with system rankings. The study releases all generated relevance judgments, code, and experimental results to establish a baseline for future research and promote a level playing field for LLM-based evaluation. The paper introduces methods for assessing the alignment of various judgment approaches to both human label orders and standard IR evaluation metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its systematic and comparative analysis of a diverse set of LLM-based relevance judgment methods, going beyond the typical focus on replicating graded human judgments. It provides a broader perspective by incorporating binary, graded, pairwise preference, and nugget-based approaches. The explicit comparison of different LLM relevance judgement methods and the release of the generated data is novel. The proposed methodologies for measuring alignment and agreement are also a significant contribution. The method for comparing different relevance judgements on a level playing field is very useful.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from addressing crucial challenges in using LLMs for IR evaluation.  Automating relevance assessment is becoming increasingly important. The paper provides a benchmark for various assessment methods for future work. The paper addresses concerns about biases and inconsistencies in LLM-based evaluation by proposing methodologies for comparing LLM assessments. By providing these metrics, the paper provides more standardized methods for evaluating these models.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Comparison:** The paper's strength is its thorough comparison of multiple LLM-based relevance assessment methods, enabling a more informed selection of methods for specific evaluation needs.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology for evaluating alignment with human labels and agreement with system rankings provides a practical framework for comparing different assessment methods.
*   **Reproducibility and Data Release:** The release of code, data, and experimental results ensures reproducibility and provides a valuable resource for the IR community.
*   **Use of Different LLMs**: Inclusion of data generated by Llama 3.2b adds value.
*   **Standardization:** The establishment of a common evaluation methodology, especially with compatibility as a unifying metric, promotes rigorous comparison of methods.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited LLM Perspective:** While GPT-4o results are shown, the absence of the Llama 3.2b results from the paper itself (but available in the repository) limits the discussion of the influence of the LLM architecture on the performance of different methods.
*   **Limited Dataset Variety:** Datasets used are TREC-based (passage retrieval) except ANTIQUE which is question answering, the inclusion of other collections such as conversational search would strengthen the generalizability of the conclusions.
*   **Depth of analysis:** Some of the analysis is presented at a very high-level, e.g., the influence of different aggregation strategies in nugget-based methods could have been analyzed in more detail.
*   **Discussion section**: Additional discussion concerning the implications of the differences between these judgements would be helpful.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of IR evaluation by:

*   Providing a practical guide for selecting appropriate LLM-based relevance assessment methods.
*   Enabling more rigorous and standardized comparisons of different LLM-based evaluation methods.
*   Facilitating the development of more robust and reliable LLM-based evaluation metrics.
*   Opening avenues for future research in addressing the limitations of LLM-based evaluation, such as biases and inconsistencies.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8.  The paper offers important advancements in a rapidly evolving field. Its novelty lies in its systematic comparison of various LLM-based relevance judgment methods and the introduction of methods for evaluating alignment and agreement. It is not perfect but it provides a significant contribution to the IR community and it promotes further research in LLM-based relevance evaluation.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### CDF-RAG: Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12560v1)
- **Authors**: Elahe Khatibi, Ziyu Wang, Amir M. Rahmani
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has significantly enhanced large language models (LLMs) in knowledge-intensive tasks by incorporating external knowledge retrieval. However, existing RAG frameworks primarily rely on semantic similarity and correlation-driven retrieval, limiting their ability to distinguish true causal relationships from spurious associations. This results in responses that may be factually grounded but fail to establish cause-and-effect mechanisms, leading to incomplete or misleading insights. To address this issue, we introduce Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation (CDF-RAG), a framework designed to improve causal consistency, factual accuracy, and explainability in generative reasoning. CDF-RAG iteratively refines queries, retrieves structured causal graphs, and enables multi-hop causal reasoning across interconnected knowledge sources. Additionally, it validates responses against causal pathways, ensuring logically coherent and factually grounded outputs. We evaluate CDF-RAG on four diverse datasets, demonstrating its ability to improve response accuracy and causal correctness over existing RAG-based methods. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/ elakhatibi/CDF-RAG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CDF-RAG (Causal Dynamic Feedback for Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a novel framework designed to enhance Large Language Models (LLMs) in knowledge-intensive tasks requiring causal reasoning.  CDF-RAG addresses the limitations of traditional RAG systems by integrating reinforcement learning (RL)-based query refinement, multi-hop causal graph retrieval, and alignment-based hallucination detection into a dynamic reasoning loop. It constructs a directed causal knowledge graph from text data, enabling multi-hop reasoning over causal mechanisms. The query refinement component uses RL to generate optimized queries for causal retrieval. A dual-path retrieval strategy combines semantic vector search with causal graph traversal, and a Causal Graph Check verifies the generated response against causal principles. The authors evaluate CDF-RAG on four QA datasets, demonstrating improved accuracy, causal correctness, and interpretability compared to existing RAG methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its holistic integration of several techniques into a single, cohesive framework specifically designed for causal reasoning. While individual components like query refinement, causal graph retrieval, and hallucination detection exist, their combination and orchestration within CDF-RAG is a significant contribution. The use of reinforcement learning to dynamically adapt query refinement strategies based on causal factors is particularly innovative.
*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial shortcoming of standard RAG systems – their inability to distinguish between correlation and causation. This limitation hinders their performance in tasks requiring deeper understanding and reasoning. By explicitly incorporating causal knowledge and reasoning into the retrieval and generation processes, CDF-RAG has the potential to significantly improve the trustworthiness and explainability of LLM-generated responses in areas like medical decision-making, scientific research, and policy analysis.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** CDF-RAG is a well-designed and meticulously implemented framework that tackles the challenges of causal reasoning in RAG systems.
    *   **Effective Integration:** The integration of RL-based query refinement, causal graph retrieval, and hallucination detection is well-executed and demonstrates strong synergistic effects.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling empirical evidence across diverse datasets, demonstrating consistent improvements in accuracy, causal correctness, and interpretability compared to existing methods.
    *   **Thorough Ablation Study:**  The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of each component to the overall performance of the framework.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Causal Graphs:** The framework relies on the availability of structured causal knowledge, which may not be readily available or complete in all domains. While the paper describes a method for constructing causal graphs from text data, its effectiveness depends on the quality and quantity of available textual resources.
    *   **Computational Overhead:**  The incorporation of query refinement, causal graph traversal, and hallucination detection undoubtedly adds computational overhead, potentially limiting the scalability of the framework in real-time or resource-constrained settings. The GPT-4 based validation of causal pairs also incurs significant cost.
    *   **Limited Generalization to Heterogeneous Queries:** While RL improves query refinement, the system's ability to generalize to highly diverse or informal queries may be limited. More investigation is needed to determine the extent to which our approach can address the complexities of real-world user queries.

*   **Potential Influence:** CDF-RAG has the potential to influence the direction of RAG research by highlighting the importance of causal reasoning and providing a concrete framework for incorporating it into RAG systems. The paper could also inspire further research into more efficient methods for constructing and traversing causal graphs, as well as more computationally efficient methods for hallucination detection. The approach of training agents to refine queries dynamically and aligning knowledge and retrieval has the potential to inform future research.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of RAG, some limitations prevent it from reaching a higher score. While the integration of several existing methods into a single pipeline is the novelty, the methods themselves are not revolutionary. Also, the dependency on causal graphs and the significant computational overhead associated with certain components (particularly the GPT-4 based check) are important constraints that need to be addressed. Given the comprehensive framework, strong empirical results, and potential for influencing future research, a score of 7.5 seems appropriate.

Score: 7.5
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ZeroSumEval: Scaling LLM Evaluation with Inter-Model Competition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12562v1)
- **Authors**: Haidar Khan, Hisham A. Alyahya, Yazeed Alnumay, M Saiful Bari, Bülent Yener
- **Abstract**: Evaluating the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) has traditionally relied on static benchmark datasets, human assessments, or model-based evaluations - methods that often suffer from overfitting, high costs, and biases. ZeroSumEval is a novel competition-based evaluation protocol that leverages zero-sum games to assess LLMs with dynamic benchmarks that resist saturation. ZeroSumEval encompasses a diverse suite of games, including security challenges (PyJail), classic games (Chess, Liar's Dice, Poker), knowledge tests (MathQuiz), and persuasion challenges (Gandalf, Debate). These games are designed to evaluate a range of AI capabilities such as strategic reasoning, planning, knowledge application, and creativity. Building upon recent studies that highlight the effectiveness of game-based evaluations for LLMs, ZeroSumEval enhances these approaches by providing a standardized and extensible framework. To demonstrate this, we conduct extensive experiments with >7000 simulations across 7 games and 13 models. Our results show that while frontier models from the GPT and Claude families can play common games and answer questions, they struggle to play games that require creating novel and challenging questions. We also observe that models cannot reliably jailbreak each other and fail generally at tasks requiring creativity. We release our code at https://github.com/facebookresearch/ZeroSumEval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "ZEROSUMEVAL: Scaling LLM Evaluation with Inter-Model Competition" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZeroSumEval, a novel benchmark framework for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) using zero-sum game competitions. The framework aims to address limitations in existing LLM evaluation methods such as overfitting, data contamination, prompt sensitivity, and lack of scalability. ZeroSumEval encompasses a diverse set of games including security challenges (PyJail), classic games (Chess, Liar's Dice, Poker), knowledge tests (MathQuiz), and persuasion challenges (Gandalf, Debate). The core idea is that the competitive setting with dynamically generated challenges (e.g., in MathQuiz or PyJail) provides a more robust and informative assessment of LLM capabilities than static datasets. The authors conduct experiments with over 7000 simulations across 7 games and 13 models. The results demonstrate that while frontier models perform well on common games, they struggle with creative tasks like generating novel and challenging questions in MathQuiz, or in the security challenges in PyJail. They also note limitations in models reliably jailbreaking each other.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using zero-sum games to evaluate LLMs isn't entirely new. Other works have explored games as benchmarks. However, ZeroSumEval's contribution lies in providing a *standardized and extensible framework* to incorporate a diverse set of games *along with a novel scalability of self-play for validation*. The framework is also notable for the focus on *dynamic benchmark generation* within some of the games, designed to resist saturation and overfitting.

*   **Significance:**  The potential impact is significant.  Current LLM benchmarks often struggle to keep pace with rapidly evolving models.  ZeroSumEval's dynamic and competitive approach offers a path towards more robust, scalable, and less easily gamed evaluations. The framework offers a way to stress-test LLMs in scenarios requiring strategic reasoning, creativity, and knowledge application, capabilities that are increasingly important for real-world applications. The framework is open-source which promotes community adoption and extensions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scalability:** The framework allows for easily scaling the evaluation process through simulation. This is a major advantage over human-annotated benchmarks.
    *   **Dynamic benchmarks:** Some of the games dynamically generate challenges which reduces the chances of overfitting.
    *   **Interpretability:** Game traces can reveal model strengths and weaknesses more effectively than traditional metrics.
    *   **Use of DSPy:** The use of DSPy is smart and reduces sensitivity to prompt variation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** Running thousands of game simulations is likely to be computationally expensive, which could limit accessibility for some researchers.
    *   **Game Specificity:** The framework's value is heavily dependent on the design of the games themselves. While the authors cover a good range of AI capabilities, there might be other important aspects that are not well captured. Games are *engineered*. While this is what provides experimental control, it also puts a constraint on what can be uncovered from experimentation. The complexity of games may need to evolve to continually challenge models and require effort in new game design.
    *   **Evaluation Reliance on Other LLMs:** The reliance on LLMs for verification (e.g. determining if a created math question is valid) introduces a potential bias and limits the evaluation's objectivity.
    *   **Lack of Human Correlation:** While the competitive setting offers advantages, the paper lacks a thorough investigation of how well ZeroSumEval correlates with human evaluations. This would strengthen the argument that ZeroSumEval is a meaningful measure of real-world performance.

*   **Justification for Score:** I assign a score of 7. The paper has a well-motivated approach to LLM evaluation by leveraging zero-sum games, however the novelty, while present, is not groundbreaking, given prior explorations of games in this space. The main contribution lies in the design of a standardized and extensible framework and providing strong evidence for its scalability and dynamic nature. The lack of human correlation studies and potential computational cost associated with high-volume simulations are the main limitations.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt-Driven and Training-Free Forgetting Approach and Dataset for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12574v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Yu, Mohd Yamani Inda Idris, Pei Wang
- **Abstract**: The widespread adoption of diffusion models in image generation has increased the demand for privacy-compliant unlearning. However, due to the high-dimensional nature and complex feature representations of diffusion models, achieving selective unlearning remains challenging, as existing methods struggle to remove sensitive information while preserving the consistency of non-sensitive regions. To address this, we propose an Automatic Dataset Creation Framework based on prompt-based layered editing and training-free local feature removal, constructing the ForgetMe dataset and introducing the Entangled evaluation metric. The Entangled metric quantifies unlearning effectiveness by assessing the similarity and consistency between the target and background regions and supports both paired (Entangled-D) and unpaired (Entangled-S) image data, enabling unsupervised evaluation. The ForgetMe dataset encompasses a diverse set of real and synthetic scenarios, including CUB-200-2011 (Birds), Stanford-Dogs, ImageNet, and a synthetic cat dataset. We apply LoRA fine-tuning on Stable Diffusion to achieve selective unlearning on this dataset and validate the effectiveness of both the ForgetMe dataset and the Entangled metric, establishing them as benchmarks for selective unlearning. Our work provides a scalable and adaptable solution for advancing privacy-preserving generative AI.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and a score.

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel approach to selective unlearning in large language models (specifically focusing on generative image models). It proposes an automatic dataset creation framework that relies on prompt-based layered editing and training-free local feature removal. This framework constructs the *ForgetMe* dataset, which contains a diverse set of real and synthetic images specifically designed for evaluating selective forgetting. Crucially, the paper introduces the *Entangled* evaluation metric, which quantifies unlearning effectiveness by assessing the similarity and consistency between target and background regions, supporting both paired and unpaired image data for unsupervised evaluation.  The method fine-tunes Stable Diffusion using LoRA and validates the effectiveness of the dataset and metric. The authors release the dataset and code to encourage further research in privacy-preserving generative AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper exhibits some novelty in its approach to selective unlearning.

*   **Automatic Dataset Creation:** The automatic dataset creation framework is a significant contribution. It addresses a gap in existing research by providing a scalable and adaptable method to generate datasets tailored for unlearning evaluation. The use of prompt-based layered editing and training-free feature removal is a unique combination.
*   **Entangled Metric:** The introduction of the *Entangled* metric is a key contribution. While other metrics exist for evaluating generative models, this metric specifically targets the nuances of unlearning, considering both the removal of target features and the preservation of background consistency. The distinction between *Entangled-D* and *Entangled-S* for paired and unpaired data is also noteworthy.
*   **Combination of Techniques:** Integrating techniques like SAM, LaMa, and GPT for dataset creation in a training-free manner demonstrates a solid engineering effort. The dataset combines both real and synthetic data, which enables for a robust evaluation on the model performance.

*Significance and Weaknesses:*

*   **Focus on Image Generation:** The paper is heavily focused on image generation, which limits its direct applicability to other domains like natural language processing. While the core concepts might be transferable, the specific implementation is tightly coupled with the image generation setting.
*   **Reliance on Existing Models:** The framework relies on pre-trained models like Stable Diffusion, SAM, LaMa, and GPT. The overall performance is contingent on the capabilities and biases of these existing models. While this is a practical approach, it also inherits the limitations of the underlying models.
*   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The paper is more focused on practical implementation and empirical validation than on theoretical analysis. While the *Entangled* metric is well-defined, there is no deeper exploration of its theoretical properties or limitations. There is also a limited discussion around which areas of the pre-trained models are actually changed by LoRA tuning.
*   **Scaling Challenges:** While the automatic dataset creation framework is a plus, the manual refinement steps of refining prompts may not scale well to very large datasets or more complex scenarios.
*   **Limited Exploration of Unlearning Methods:** The paper primarily uses LoRA fine-tuning as the unlearning method. It would be more significant if it compared different unlearning methods (e.g., parameter pruning, adversarial training) using the proposed dataset and metric, demonstrating the generality of the framework.
*   **Generalizability:** The presented results and comparisons might not be generalizable to other diffusion models, because the results are only specific for the fine-tuned model.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence research in privacy-preserving generative AI. The *ForgetMe* dataset and the *Entangled* metric provide valuable benchmarks for evaluating selective unlearning techniques. The automatic dataset creation framework can serve as a template for generating unlearning datasets in other domains.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the above analysis, the paper offers a contribution that advances the practical aspects of evaluating unlearning in image generation. However, the limitations in theoretical depth, generalizability, and unlearning methods, warrant a more moderate assessment.

Score: 6

**Rationale:**

*   The dataset and metric contributions are valuable additions to the field, making the paper a 6.
*   The approach's reliance on pre-trained models and its limited theoretical exploration prevent it from achieving a higher score. A deeper analysis would improve the long-term impact and significance.
*   More complete experiments would result in a better and well rounded result.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Identifying and Mitigating the Influence of the Prior Distribution in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12585v1)
- **Authors**: Liyi Zhang, Veniamin Veselovsky, R. Thomas McCoy, Thomas L. Griffiths
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) sometimes fail to respond appropriately to deterministic tasks -- such as counting or forming acronyms -- because the implicit prior distribution they have learned over sequences of tokens influences their responses. In this work, we show that, in at least some cases, LLMs actually compute the information needed to perform these tasks correctly, and we identify some interventions that can allow them to access this information to improve their performance. First, we show that simply prompting the language model to not rely on its prior knowledge leads to dramatic improvements in prior-dominated tasks. We then use mechanistic interpretability techniques to localize the prior within the LLM and manipulate the extent to which that prior influences its responses. Specifically, we show that it is possible to identify layers of the underlying neural network that correlate with the prior probability of a response and that lightweight finetuning of these layers with basic prompts on prior-dominated tasks achieves high performance on held-out answers. These results suggest that the information required to produce a correct response is contained within the representations of the problems formed by the models. Furthermore, we show that this finetuning is significantly more effective for prior-dominated tasks, and that the error after finetuning is no longer correlated with the prior. Our results suggest that it may be possible to define effective methods for manipulating the extent to which LLMs rely upon their priors in solving problems, potentially increasing their performance in settings where LLMs hallucinate for reasons related to the prior probability of token sequences.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the influence of learned prior distributions on the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in deterministic tasks.  It argues that LLMs sometimes fail on these tasks (e.g., counting, acronym formation) not because they lack the required information, but because their inherent prior distribution over token sequences leads them to favor more common outputs, even if incorrect. The authors demonstrate this effect and explore methods to mitigate it. They find that simply prompting the model to ignore its prior can improve performance. More significantly, they identify specific layers within the LLM that correlate with the prior probability of a response. They then show that lightweight finetuning of these layers, using a stratified approach to avoid overfitting, can significantly improve performance on tasks dominated by the prior. Further, they show that after such fine-tuning, errors are no longer correlated with prior probability, indicating that the fine-tuning process effectively reduces the influence of the prior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers moderate novelty.  The idea that LLMs' performance is affected by priors is not new (McCoy et al., 2023 is a direct antecedent). However, the specific contributions lie in the methods employed to analyze and mitigate this influence.

    *   The use of logit lens to identify layers associated with the prior is a valuable application of mechanistic interpretability.
    *   The demonstration that simple prompting can help is a useful, practical finding.
    *   The stratified finetuning approach to reduce the prior's influence, specifically targeting layers identified through logit lens, is a substantial contribution.

*   **Significance:**  The findings have important implications for improving LLMs' reliability in tasks where precise output is required, going beyond fluent but potentially inaccurate text generation.  The ability to reduce the influence of the prior has the potential to be helpful in addressing hallucinations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear and well-defined problem:** The paper clearly defines the issue of prior influence and its impact on deterministic tasks.
    *   **Methodological rigor:** The authors use established techniques (logit lens, finetuning, prompting) in a thoughtful and well-controlled manner. The stratified train/validation split is crucial for avoiding overfitting and demonstrating the presence of task-relevant knowledge within the model.
    *   **Demonstration of effectiveness:** The empirical results convincingly show that their interventions improve performance and reduce the correlation between errors and prior probability.
    *   **Open-source model usage**: Using an open-source LLM such as LLAMA facilitates reproducibility and wider adoption of their findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope of tasks:** The focus on a relatively small set of tasks (counting, acronyms, shift ciphers) limits the generalizability of the findings. While these tasks are illustrative, it's unclear how well the techniques would transfer to more complex and diverse settings.
    *   **Lack of comparison to alternative methods:** The paper mentions but doesn't fully explore other techniques for mitigating prior influence. A more thorough comparison would strengthen the claims about the effectiveness of their proposed approach. The lack of success using prior-context steering or unigram ablations (described in the appendix) is insufficient, as these approaches are themselves very recent and may not be the best method for prior manipulation.
    *   **Limited theoretical analysis:**  While the empirical results are strong, the paper lacks a deeper theoretical explanation for why the interventions work.  Understanding the underlying mechanisms would increase the impact of the research. What specific types of knowledge are being unlocked by the fine-tuning process? How does prompting influence internal activations?
    *   **Layer selection for fine-tuning is somewhat ad-hoc**: The process of selecting the layers to fine-tune based on correlation with the prior distribution could be more systematic. Further, the paper only explores fine-tuning a single layer - it is very possible that fine-tuning multiple layers at the same time may further boost performance.

*   **Impact:** The work has the potential to influence how LLMs are trained and used in situations where accuracy is paramount. The techniques for identifying and mitigating prior influence could be incorporated into training pipelines or used as post-hoc interventions to improve performance on specific tasks.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the work builds upon existing knowledge about the influence of priors on LLM behavior, it offers a valuable contribution by demonstrating effective methods for analyzing and mitigating this influence. The stratified fine-tuning approach, in particular, appears promising and warrants further investigation. However, the limited scope of the tasks, the lack of a comprehensive theoretical analysis, and the limited comparison to alternatives keep this from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Simplifying Graph Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12588v1)
- **Authors**: Liheng Ma, Soumyasundar Pal, Yingxue Zhang, Philip H. S. Torr, Mark Coates
- **Abstract**: Transformers have attained outstanding performance across various modalities, employing scaled-dot-product (SDP) attention mechanisms. Researchers have attempted to migrate Transformers to graph learning, but most advanced Graph Transformers are designed with major architectural differences, either integrating message-passing or incorporating sophisticated attention mechanisms. These complexities prevent the easy adoption of Transformer training advances. We propose three simple modifications to the plain Transformer to render it applicable to graphs without introducing major architectural distortions. Specifically, we advocate for the use of (1) simplified $L_2$ attention to measure the magnitude closeness of tokens; (2) adaptive root-mean-square normalization to preserve token magnitude information; and (3) a relative positional encoding bias with a shared encoder. Significant performance gains across a variety of graph datasets justify the effectiveness of our proposed modifications. Furthermore, empirical evaluation on the expressiveness benchmark reveals noteworthy realized expressiveness in the graph isomorphism.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the increasing complexity of Graph Transformers, which often deviate significantly from the standard Transformer architecture through the incorporation of message-passing networks (MPNNs) or sophisticated attention mechanisms.  These modifications complicate the adoption of Transformer training advances and impede cross-modality unification.  The authors propose three simple modifications to the plain Transformer to make it applicable to graph data without major architectural changes: (1) Simplified L2 attention, which measures magnitude closeness; (2) Adaptive root-mean-square normalization (AdaRMSN), designed to preserve token magnitude information; and (3) Relative positional encoding with a shared encoder.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of these modifications through experiments on various graph datasets and an expressiveness benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of simplifying Graph Transformers and bringing them closer to the original Transformer architecture has some merit. The individual proposed modifications, however, possess varying degrees of novelty.
    *   *Simplified L2 Attention:* While the modification is simple to implement, the idea of using Euclidean distance in the attention mechanism isn't entirely new, but the integration of this with scaled dot product attention as an offset *is* a slightly new take.
    *   *Adaptive RMSN:* This is more novel than the first modification, as it explicitly tries to address the limitation of information loss of magnitude in tokens that normal RMSN has. The proposed parameterization is fairly simple, making it easy to implement and more efficient.
    *   *Relative positional encoding with a shared encoder:*  This is also not entirely new but there are slight differences.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper lies in its attempt to bridge the gap between standard Transformers and Graph Transformers. If the proposed modifications consistently deliver strong performance without the architectural complexity of previous approaches, it could facilitate the easier transfer of advances from the broader Transformer research.
    *The idea of simplifying Graph Transformers is a step in the right direction. In the past couple of years, there has been an increasing number of Graph Transformers proposed but most of them are too complicated.* The results show better performance and expressiveness, which is good. However, *if the results were not up to par*, the significance of this paper would become very low.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Simplicity:* The key strength is the simplicity of the proposed modifications.  This makes them easy to implement, integrate into existing Transformer frameworks, and understand. This could potentially ease the adoption of training advances in general Transformers as well as encourage others to propose more simpler versions.
    *   *Empirical Results:* The empirical results on various datasets are compelling, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed modifications.
    *   *Theoretical Discussion:* The paper contains a clear theoretical motivation for the modifications, explaining why they address specific limitations of plain Transformers on graph data.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Incremental Improvements:* The modifications can be viewed as incremental improvements over existing techniques rather than radical innovations. This doesn't diminish their value, but it limits the paper's overall impact.
    *   *Limited Scope of Evaluation:* While the datasets used are representative, a more extensive evaluation across diverse graph learning tasks would strengthen the conclusions.  Specifically, assessing the model's scalability to very large graphs is crucial.
    *   *Ablation Studies:* A more thorough ablation study, considering combinations of the proposed modifications, would further illuminate their individual contributions.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to influence future research on Graph Transformers by encouraging a focus on simplicity and alignment with standard Transformer architectures. If follow-up works build upon these modifications and demonstrate their effectiveness in diverse settings, the paper's influence could grow. The paper *might* also influence models that utilize multimodal foundation models.

**Justification for Score**

The paper has good empirical results and a sound rationale that demonstrates effectiveness of SGT, but the novelty of the proposed architecture is incremental. Some of the proposed techniques have been around for a while (positional embeddings) and others are just simplifications of the traditional Graph Transformer.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### GeoSense: Evaluating Identification and Application of Geometric Principles in Multimodal Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12597v1)
- **Authors**: Liangyu Xu, Yingxiu Zhao, Jingyun Wang, Yingyao Wang, Bu Pi, Chen Wang, Mingliang Zhang, Jihao Gu, Xiang Li, Xiaoyong Zhu, Jun Song, Bo Zheng
- **Abstract**: Geometry problem-solving (GPS), a challenging task requiring both visual comprehension and symbolic reasoning, effectively measures the reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Humans exhibit strong reasoning ability in this task through accurate identification and adaptive application of geometric principles within visual contexts. However, existing benchmarks fail to jointly assess both dimensions of the human-like geometric reasoning mechanism in MLLMs, remaining a critical gap in assessing their ability to tackle GPS. To this end, we introduce GeoSense, the first comprehensive bilingual benchmark designed to systematically evaluate the geometric reasoning abilities of MLLMs through the lens of geometric principles. GeoSense features a five-level hierarchical framework of geometric principles spanning plane and solid geometry, an intricately annotated dataset of 1,789 problems, and an innovative evaluation strategy. Through extensive experiments on GeoSense with various open-source and closed-source MLLMs, we observe that Gemini-2.0-pro-flash performs best, achieving an overall score of $65.3$. Our in-depth analysis reveals that the identification and application of geometric principles remain a bottleneck for leading MLLMs, jointly hindering their reasoning abilities. These findings underscore GeoSense's potential to guide future advancements in MLLMs' geometric reasoning capabilities, paving the way for more robust and human-like reasoning in artificial intelligence.
